# Imrei Binah: a meditative reading

---
*Unity Index, meditative layer, 2026-09-29. This file reads the Mitteler Rebbe's Imrei Binah (printed 1821; file lines 1-603) for what it is when you contemplate it, not for what it argues. The map was the line-by-line unity reading in `../imrei-binah/` (lines 1-98, 99-181, 182-265, 266-351, 352-437, 438-521, 522-603) and `../IMREI-BINAH.md`; every section was then read again in the source, `Chassidus-txt/R. Dovber Schneuri (Mitteler Rebbe) (1773-1827)/Imrei Binah (Lubavitch, 1821).txt`. Each file line is one printed column, often thousands of characters long, so a "line" below can hold a whole paragraph. The book has an introduction and four gates: Petach HaShaar (the Opening of the Gate, sections 1-26), Shaar Kriat Shema (the Gate of the Shema, sections 1-102), Shaar HaTzitzit (sections 1-23) and Shaar HaTefillin (sections 24-41); this reading follows those sections, and where the file has lost a section number the unit is named by the numbers around it. Every Hebrew line below was checked with `find_he.py verify` at the line given and came back FOUND, and was checked again, letters only, to sit on that line itself. The text has no vowel points, and its abbreviations and misprints are quoted as printed. The ladder sittings (L01-L14) are those of `../manual/part2.json`. The JSON twin is `imrei-binah.json`.*

## 1. The book as one meditative journey

Imrei Binah is one long walk through a single verse and the two mitzvot bound to it. "Hear, O Israel, Havayah is our God, Havayah is One": the book asks what happens when a person says it, and it keeps asking for six hundred columns. It begins by clearing the table. The Alter Rebbe, the author says, taught only one thing, to fix "the simple unity of God" in the mind and heart of every Jew (line 3), and God "does not want" many clever intentions, "only one intention": to bind your soul to Him Himself (line 6). It also takes away the excuse that this is too high. The unity dwells only in bitul, in not feeling yourself (line 6), and that door is open to anyone.

The Opening of the Gate (Petach HaShaar) teaches the reader how to hold the word "One". There are two ways of saying it, from above and from below, and they hang on each other (line 11). The drawn-out dalet is where "their beginning is wedged in their end" (line 22): the low place holds the high. Night comes before morning, and the darkness is there because a greater light must come (line 36). The work of this world is to bring out the hidden places of the heart (line 46). By the end the reader is told to "know today": as there is no other in the heavens, so there is no other on the earth (line 90).

The Gate of the Shema is the long middle, and it goes word by word. "Our God" is the inside of a cup, where the drink and the cup become one (line 153). "Echad" opens onto "Yachid", the One who is alone and not within number (line 122). "Baruch Shem" brings it down into the world, and the book makes one of its boldest claims: the lower unity has an advantage over the higher, because the earth, the end of all, holds "the power of the beginning" (line 179). The soul was sown in a body so that it could weep, and that weeping reaches deeper than the delight it had above (line 185). Then the three loves of "with all your heart, soul and might"; the running and the return, where "the return is the main thing" (line 283); rain, bread, wine and oil as pictures of Torah; the letters of "echad" and "va'ed"; the silence of a scholar who has not yet found the answer (line 389). The gate ends by weighing study against deed, and refuses to settle it once for all: study is greater now, the deed in the time to come, and learning the laws of a mitzvah draws the Essence into its root (lines 437, 455).

The Gate of Tzitzit wraps the reader in a garment. "He wraps Himself in light as a garment" means both a covering and a bright light spread out, and both are true (lines 469, 502). The blue thread burns evil away; the white threads turn it; and then the book ranks them again: true teshuvah "makes good out of evil itself" (line 514), like death becoming life (line 530). The Gate of Tefillin gives the last picture, the seal. What we bring up shapes what comes down: "spirit brings spirit, just like it" (line 571). Love and excitement are a "something" and draw down light in garments; bitul, becoming like a silent stone before the King, draws down the Essence with no garment at all (lines 561, 572). The body bowing in the Amidah is higher than the bitul of mind and heart (line 562), and the plain physical deed is the only vessel the future has (line 564).

So the journey goes from one intention, to one word, to one verse lived in the body. It starts in the mind, and it keeps pushing the mind to know rather than only believe. But it ends somewhere past the mind: standing in the Amidah "like one who stands before someone much greater" (line 602), in a prayer that is "a taste of the future" (line 603). The book stops there, in the middle of a sentence. For a person who contemplates it, that is almost right: the last thing it teaches is to stand still and let Him be revealed.

## 2. Unit by unit

*Skipped, with nothing to contemplate: the book's title lines (1-2), the headings of Petach HaShaar (9-10), of the Gate of the Shema (91-92, with the colophon of Petach HaShaar), of the Gate of Tzitzit (465-466, with the colophon of the Gate of the Shema) and of the Gate of Tefillin (546). The author's signature (line 8) is folded into the Introduction unit. Shaar Kriat Shema §76 (lines 374-376): a letter-map of the large letters and a study of how a person deliberates before he speaks; its point ("ed", witness) is carried in §77.*

## Introduction (the author's preface) (lines 1-8)

**The movement.** It clears the table. Before any teaching it takes away the many clever intentions a person might bring to the Shema and leaves one: to bind yourself to Him Himself. It also takes away the excuse "this is too high for me".

> כי לא חפץ ה' באלה כ"א בכונה אחת לבד לקשר נפשו אל אמתת עצמותו ית' דוקא
> *For God does not want these, only one intention alone: to bind one's soul to the truth of His very Essence.*

**Contemplating it.** Start wide. The Mitteler Rebbe says his father taught, every Shabbat, in public and in private, one thing only: to fix "the simple unity of God, the Essence of the Infinite", in the mind and heart of every single person, each by his own measure (line 3). Then step by step. The kabbalists built long ladders of intentions, names and unions for the Shema. The author does not call them false. He says they all rise to one place, and that a person can climb them and still miss the point, collecting "many calculations" to feed his own soul (line 6). Then to the root. What God wants is not the ladder but the one who climbs it, bound to Him, not to His qualities. And the unity lives only where a person does not feel himself at all: "the lower the soul is, the more the Infinite dwells there" (line 6). Now bring it home. You too carry a pile of intentions into prayer: to feel something, to be good at it, to get through it, to ask for things. Set them on the table and leave one. The book's picture is a man seeking "secrets" for himself, saying "my secret is mine" (line 6). It breaks because the secret here is not a hidden fact you could own. It is a Person you can only be joined to. And he closes the door on false humility. "Who am I to come to deep contemplation?" is, he says, the advice of the evil inclination (line 5). Every Jew, "even one who only knows how to read", must toil at this by his own strength.

**From inside.** A simplifying. The many threads go slack and one draws tight. Often a small relief, as if you were allowed to stop performing. And a kind of lowness that does not feel like defeat: the text's own words are "crushed and low of spirit", the place where He says He dwells.

**At the edge.** It points there at once. The one intention is to the "truth of His Essence", above every quality and name, and the only vessel for it is bitul, "no feeling of oneself at all" (line 6). The mind cannot hold the Essence. It can only stop holding itself.

**Into the day.** Before the Shema today, say one plain sentence in your own words: "I want You, not something from You."

## Petach HaShaar §1: the two ways of saying "One" (lines 11-12)

**The movement.** It gives the mind two directions at once. Going up, everything melts into its source. Coming down, He is made king in every corner of the world. Then it says the two are one road.

> שני אופני היחודים הנ"ל ענין א' הוא והא בהא תליא
> *These two ways of unity are one matter, and each hangs on the other.*

**Contemplating it.** Take it wide. When you say "One" in the Shema, the kabbalists say: let everything rise and be absorbed in Him, "to Him and not to His qualities". The Sages say: make Him king "in heaven and earth and the four directions of the world". One is ascent, one is descent. Step by step, the letters of the word "echad" carry the map. The chet is the seven heavens and the earth. The dalet is the four directions. The alef is the first source that lights them all, "the master of the world" (line 11). Then the root. Alef turned around spells pele, "wonder". When wisdom is still hidden inside what is above it, it is a wonder no one can place. When it comes out, it becomes a teacher and a source. And the text says the two are one: "He is the place of the world, and the world is not His place" is true both ways at the same time (line 12). So bring it to your own room. First let the walls, the floor, the street outside go quiet before Him, as if they were not there. Then, without leaving that, see Him as king of this very room, this floor, these four walls. The picture is a flame bound to a coal. It holds for the first direction. It breaks for the second, because a flame does not come back down to rule the coal. Here He does.

**From inside.** A doubleness that does not tear. First a hush, the world gone thin before Him; then a filling, the same world standing firm under His rule. Some find the second harder than the first.

**At the edge.** The first direction reaches pele, the wonder that "counts as the Essence" (line 12), where the six directions vanish. The mind cannot stand there; it can only give itself over, which is why the text says this moment of the Shema asks for self-giving.

**Into the day.** When you enter a room today, name it silently: "You are King here too."

## Petach HaShaar §2: light and dark still mixed in wisdom (lines 13-15)

**The movement.** It takes the mind back before things split into this and that. Kindness and severity, light and dark, heaven and earth begin together in one hidden point and only later come apart.

> טרם שבאה אור דחכמה מן ההעלם דאור הכתר לגלוי יש היה האור והחשך כלולים יחד ולא היה הבדלה ביניהם כלל
> *Before the light of wisdom came out of hiding into being, light and darkness were included together, with no separation between them at all.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth first: "In the beginning" is wisdom, and in it "all were made together", heaven and earth and all their hosts, before the first "let there be light" (line 13). Step by step: light is kindness coming down, darkness is holding back and hiding. They split only when wisdom comes out into the open. The Sages say light and darkness were "used in a mixture" until God divided them (line 14). The text's picture is a mind at work. A new insight comes first as one simple point. Only when you start to argue it does it lean two ways, to permit and to forbid, like the schools of Shammai and Hillel who "heard one thing" and leaned apart (line 14, line 17). Bring it down. Think of a hard day: what was kind in it and what was harsh. Now go back before you sorted it. Once it was one day, from one Hand. The picture breaks because a human point of insight is small and passing. Here the point before the split is the source of the whole world.

**From inside.** The sorting mind relaxes its grip a little. Things you had filed under "good" and "bad" sit for a moment in one place. Not approval of the bad; a sense that it has not escaped Him.

**At the edge.** It stops at the edge of wisdom. Before wisdom lies the hidden light of Keter, which the text does not open here.

**Into the day.** Tonight, name one thing from today that felt harsh and say: "This too came from the one place."

**Ladder.** L08 (What can't meet in me meets in Him)

## Petach HaShaar §3: length and breadth, the point and the many words (lines 16-17)

**The movement.** It turns the mind to the one point inside all explaining. It shows how much of what we hear and say is only wrapping, needed but not the thing.

> שכל מה שמרבה בדברים אינו אלא בחי' חיצוניות וטפל לגבי עיקר נקודת השכל
> *Everything one adds in words is only outwardness, secondary to the main point of the idea.*

**Contemplating it.** This section is mostly a map of space: up and down, east and west, north and south. The mind does not need to hold the map. It needs one picture from it. The picture is a teacher and a small child. The teacher has one point, "only one word". To reach the child he must wrap it in many words and stories. Without them the child would not understand. Still, next to the point, they are "back" and not "face" (line 17). Length, says the text, is that coming down from the teacher's point to the child's ear, like a river running hundreds of miles to the sea. Breadth is the point opening sideways in its own light, many reasons for and against, all still "face" (lines 16-17). Bring it home. Your day is full of words: talk, news, messages, your own thoughts. Ask what the one point is under them. The picture breaks at the top. A teacher's point is still one idea among others. The point this book is walking toward is not an idea at all.

**From inside.** A quieting of noise. The many words become lighter, as if you could see through them. Often a longing to hear the one word under all of them.

**At the edge.** It does not reach there in this section. It is groundwork: the "point" will later be the alef, and the alef the wonder above it.

**Into the day.** When you speak to a child or a friend today, find your one point first, and say that plainly.

## Petach HaShaar §4: length, breadth, and "as above, so below" (lines 18-20)

**The movement.** It weighs two ways that goodness travels and then sets above both a third way: the whole of the giver arriving whole in the receiver. The mind is taught to want that third way.

> אלא כמו שהוא למעל' תבא למט' זהו הנק' מעל' ומט' שהוא העיקר והעצם
> *Rather, as it is above, so it comes below; this is what is called "above and below", which is the main thing and the essence.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: every gift spreads in two ways. It comes down in a line, from the teacher's point to the child, getting smaller but never leaving the truth. Or it spreads sideways into many reasons, rich and inward, but able to lean too far to kindness or to judgment (line 19). Length has its advantage and breadth has its own. Neither is the whole. The whole is "above and below": the giver's fullness arriving in the receiver as it is, without being cut down into point, line and plane (line 19). The text's picture is a student who receives "the whole essence" of his teacher's flow, with no hiding and no steps (line 20). And the roof and the floor of a house, "whose surface is exactly equal". Bring it to your own table. Most of what reaches you from God comes stepped down: a little light, a little help, a little sense. Picture instead one moment where what is above simply arrives, as it is. The picture breaks, and the text knows it: the author gives a second view in which "above and below" come last, after the four walls are up (line 20). He keeps both, and the next section says why.

**From inside.** A reaching past the measured portion. Not greed for more, but a sense that the whole Giver could be present, not only His gifts.

**At the edge.** It points to it. What arrives "as it is above" is not a light at all but the source itself, and the text will soon call that the Essence. Here it is still a picture.

**Into the day.** When you give someone something today, give it whole: full attention, no half-listening.

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Petach HaShaar §5: why the dalet is drawn out (lines 21-23)

**The movement.** It turns the ladder upside down. The lowest point, where all four directions end, is found to be the highest, "which came before everything". The mind that was climbing is sent down to find the top.

> והוא בחי' מטה ממש שנעוץ תחלתן בסופן והוא בחי' מעלה שקדם להכל
> *And this is the very "below", where their beginning is wedged in their end, and it is the "above" that came before everything.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: both views of space are true (line 21). The giver's flow can come down whole, above all measure, or it can come down through the four walls and then give the house its roof and floor. Step by step: the chet of "echad" is heaven and earth; the dalet is the four directions. We draw out the dalet in the Shema because all four directions gather in the last place, the flat ground of the earth, and that last place is where the beginning is fastened (line 22). The root: "Make Him king" is not a climb but a coming down, from the Essence that is above being a source at all, into heaven and earth and the four winds (line 22). And it happens every day: each day the first saying, "In the beginning", must be renewed from the hidden nothing (line 23). Bring it home. When you say the dalet of "echad" slowly, think of the floor under your feet, then the four walls around you. That is not the bottom of the ladder. The text says it is the top, bent round. The picture of the dalet, a line across and a line down, breaks because letters stand still. The text says the ground is being renewed each day.

**From inside.** A reversal that settles the body. The ground becomes a place of meeting, not exile. Some feel their ordinary life suddenly weighted, as if it counted more than they thought.

**At the edge.** It touches the edge in one phrase: the "above that came before everything". What comes before the beginning is not in the chain. The mind can point at it through the lowest thing but cannot stand in it.

**Into the day.** Say the dalet of "echad" slowly today with your feet flat on the floor, and mean the floor.

## Petach HaShaar §6: the Ark that took no space (lines 24-26)

**The movement.** It hands the mind a real object, a box of wood and gold in a measured room, that did not take up room. The mind is brought to its edge by a thing it could have touched.

> גם בהיותו במקום ומדה ונתפש שם אינו נתפס במקום זה כלל וכלל
> *Even while it was in a place and a measure, and held there, it was not held by that place at all.*

**Contemplating it.** Take it wide. The Holy of Holies was twenty cubits across. The Ark stood in it, and still there were ten cubits on every side (line 24). A thing with a size, standing in a room with a size, and taking none of it. Step by step: it is no wonder that light above place is above place. The wonder is a measured thing that is also unmeasured. "The heavens are My throne" is already a paradox, held and not held. But there it is only light that leapt down. In the Ark the Essence itself came to dwell, "a home for Him, literally" (line 25). The root: where the Essence as it is enters a bound, the bound itself becomes unbounded. "There these are not two opposites at all" (line 25). Since the Temple fell He has in His world only the four cubits of halacha, "in the place of the Ark and the tablets" (line 25). Bring it to your table. A page of the law you studied today is a measured thing. A small room where you pray is measured. The text is saying the Ark's secret can live there. The picture is a walled city. In the future, it says, Israel will be like a city without walls, open in every direction, a vessel with no edge (line 26). It breaks, since a city without walls is only large, and this is beyond large.

**From inside.** Wonder, the plain kind, like standing in front of something that should not be possible and is. Then a quiet respect for small bounded places, and for your own bounded self.

**At the edge.** Yes. The Essence "as it is" in a bound, where limit and no limit are not two (line 25). The mind cannot picture it; it can only stand, like the Ark, and not take up room.

**Into the day.** Choose one fixed spot for your prayer or study today and treat it as a small Holy of Holies.

**Ladder.** L08 (What can't meet in me meets in Him)

## Petach HaShaar §7: the great dalet, and the line that measures and is not measured (lines 27-28)

**The movement.** It shows the mind a ruler that is itself beyond measure. Everything is measured by it, and it holds both the limit and the limitless. The two unities of the Shema are found to have the same shape.

> והוא עצמו אינו מן המדה כי יש בו מעצמות אא"ס הבלתי גבול וכולל גם בחינת הגבול ב' הפכי'
> *And it itself is not of the measure, for it holds something of the Essence of the Infinite that has no bound, and it includes the bound too: two opposites.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the six words of the Shema's first verse are the higher unity; "Baruch Shem" is the lower unity; the great dalet at the end of "echad" is the kingship of God just before it goes out to make the worlds (line 27). Step by step: through all the levels runs a middle line, "the line of measure", that measures every light and vessel. That line is not itself measured. It carries the boundless and the bounded together, "as with the Ark" (line 27). Then the lower world is found to be built the same way: "as they unite above, so she unites below" (line 28). What is one above becomes one below, letter for letter, only through a screen. "In the future, Havayah is One and His Name is One, the higher and lower unity exactly equal" (line 28). Bring it home. Your day has measures: hours, amounts, rules. Think of the one who does the measuring as someone not caught in the measure. Then think of your own measured day as carrying, through that middle line, something unmeasured. The picture is a ruler. It breaks, because a ruler has its own length. This line has none of its own.

**From inside.** A steadiness. Limits stop feeling like prisons. They feel like the way the unlimited has chosen to arrive.

**At the edge.** It names the edge: "something of the Essence of the Infinite" in the very line of measure. Two opposites in one thing can be said but not pictured; the mind has to let them stand.

**Into the day.** When you look at the clock today, remember that the hours are measured by One who is not in them.

**Ladder.** L08 (What can't meet in me meets in Him)

## Petach HaShaar §8: the Shechinah in the west, and the seal in the wax (lines 29-30)

**The movement.** It turns the mind around, as in the Temple, where people bowed west, the "back" of the world. Once the gift is received, the receiver comes first. The mind learns that the end can lead.

> המקבל עיקר לאחר קבלתו השפע דנעוץ תחלתן בסופן דוקא
> *The receiver is the main thing once it has received the flow, for their beginning is wedged in their end.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: east is "front", because the light starts there. Yet in the Temple the Shechinah was in the west, and all bowed toward it, and right and left were reversed (line 29). Step by step: when a rich man gives to a poor man with his right hand, the poor man takes it facing him, and his left is opposite the giver's right. When a seal is pressed into wax, the letters come out reversed (lines 29-30). Once the flow has arrived, it follows the shape of the one who received it. The text carries this into the Torah and the calendar. The Oral Torah follows the sages' reading "even if they tell you left is right". The festivals follow the moon, which only receives from the sun (line 30). Now your own life. You are mostly a receiver: of life, of breath, of Torah. The text is saying that the receiver is not second-class. Where the gift lands and takes the receiver's shape, there the beginning is found. The picture of the seal breaks. Wax only takes an impression. Here the receiver becomes "the main thing".

**From inside.** A small dignity in being on the receiving end. Gratitude that is not humiliation. The sense that your shape matters to the One who gives.

**At the edge.** It points past itself: in the future there will be no giver and receiver at all, the moon like the sun (line 30). The text does not open that here.

**Into the day.** Receive one thing today, a meal or a kindness, facing it fully, and let it take your shape.

## Petach HaShaar §9: drawing out "echad", and evening before morning (lines 31-33)

**The movement.** It first lowers everything said so far to "the something becoming nothing", then points above it to Yachid. Then it turns to the heart and says there is no real joy that did not first pass through bitterness.

> כמו שא"א לאור בלא חשך הקדום כך א"א לאהבה ושמחה בלא מרירות והכנעה קודם
> *Just as there cannot be light without the darkness before it, so there cannot be love and joy without bitterness and surrender first.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: space itself, front and back, left and right, is only "for one who is caught in his four cubits" (line 31). For the One who made space, east is not in front. All we have said is the something going to nothing, "up to before the tzimtzum, called Yachid, above Echad" (line 32). Step by step: a day is "evening, then morning". Creation began with holding back and only then the kindness came (line 32). The angels sing at dawn because the night came first (line 33). Then the heart: prayer is nearness to God in joy. But it cannot come true without first "the utmost bitterness, lowliness and surrender", a heart bowed "depth within depth" at how far the body has pulled it from the King (line 33). The deeper the bitterness, the brighter the joy after, "like the darkness before dawn" (line 33). Bring it home. Before you pray, look honestly at one way you are far from Him today. Do not stay there. Let it be the evening that makes a morning. The picture is night and day. It breaks, because night passes on its own. The bitterness here has to be real, and it is a step, not a state to live in.

**From inside.** A sinking, then a turning. The text describes it plainly: weeping stuck in one side of the heart, and joy in the other. Afterward, a joy that is quieter and harder to shake.

**At the edge.** Yes, in a phrase: Yachid, above Echad (line 32), the level before any contraction. The text only names it here. All the ladder of bitul is still below it.

**Into the day.** Before prayer today, take one minute of honest regret, then open the siddur to a praise.

**Ladder.** L12 (Darkness turned to light)

## Petach HaShaar §10: the river of fire, the hard years of learning, the long night of exile (lines 34-36)

**The movement.** It takes the hardest parts of a life and puts them in order. Every rising is preceded by its own going down, in measure. The darkness is read as the size of the light that is coming.

> כך כל מה שיתחשך בהסת' גדול יותר זהו מפני שצריך לבא אור גדול ביותר
> *So whatever grows darker, in a greater hiding, is because a greater light must come.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: souls entering Gan Eden first immerse in a river of fire (line 34). A student reaches depth only after toil that brings him near despair (line 35). The farmer sows weeping and comes back singing. The merchant spends and loses before he gains (line 35). Even the plain kindness of bread comes after struggle, because it is called kindness, and kindness has night before it (line 35). Step by step, the text widens to a whole people. Egypt was darkness, and Sinai came out of it. This last exile is longer and darker because what comes after it is greater: the last redeemer greater than the first (line 36). Then its own picture: pregnancy. The child is hidden, curled, head between its knees. The pains that seem to hold back the birth are part of the birth. "As a woman with child draws near to give birth, she writhes and cries out" (line 36). Bring it to your own table. Name one hard thing you are inside now. The text does not say it is good. It says it has a measure, and the measure is set by what is coming. Where the picture breaks: labor has a known end. Exile does not tell you its hour. The text asks you to hold the order without knowing the time.

**From inside.** Not cheerfulness. A kind of backbone. The dark is still dark, but it is no longer meaningless. Some feel, for the first time, that their struggle is part of something being born.

**At the edge.** It points to the edge of time: "a day that is all long", the day when the sun is taken out of its sheath (line 36). The text leaves it for the next section.

**Into the day.** Say once today, about one hard thing: "This is the night before a morning."

**Ladder.** L12 (Darkness turned to light)

## Petach HaShaar §11: when the night will shine like the day (lines 37-39)

**The movement.** It breaks its own rule. After teaching that darkness always comes first, it says that in the end the darkness itself will give light. The mind is asked to hold a light that is made of hiding.

> זה יהיה עיקר החידוש לע"ל מה שבחי' הצמצום והחשך העליון יאיר
> *This will be the main new thing in the future: that the contraction, the higher darkness, will shine.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: for now, every light comes through a darkness first. But the verse says "at evening time there shall be light", and "night shines like day" (line 37). The Sages speak of a day of twenty-four hours with no night, like the day Joshua stopped the sun. Step by step, the root: when God first willed to create, it was out of "His essential kindness", the way the good wants to do good (line 38). That kindness was hidden in His Essence, and there light and darkness were one. To build a world it had to be hidden further, and so darkness came first. In the future the hidden will shine as it is. That is why the splitting of the Sea was a foretaste: "the cloud and the darkness, and it lit up the night", the darkness itself giving light (line 38). Then the text says time will change. The days will still be days, but "time will be eternal, with the eternity of the Infinite itself" (line 38). Bring it home. Think of a time in your life that was only shadow. The text says that in the end not only will light come after it; that very shadow will turn out to have been light.

**From inside.** A strange hope, deeper than comfort. Not "it will be over" but "it will have been light". Some feel a tenderness toward their own hidden years.

**At the edge.** Yes. The darkness here is "the hiding in His Essence" (line 38), and what will shine is His Essence as it is, without contraction. The mind can hold the words; it cannot picture darkness that lights up.

**Into the day.** Tonight, before sleep, say "night shines like day" and mean the darkest part of your day.

## Petach HaShaar §12: two knowings, and two hearts made one (lines 40-41)

**The movement.** It shows the mind two ways God gives, and says they will be one. Then it turns inward to two hearts, the one that loves because it understands and the one that loves because it is a soul, and asks for them to become one.

> שכאשר תעלה אהבה ורצון שבחיצוניות הלב למעלה תהי' גם היא למעלה מן הטעם והשכל
> *When the love and will of the outer heart rise up, it too will be above reason and understanding.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: God is "the God of knowings", plural (line 40). One flow comes from nothing to something, after the contraction. The other comes from "the true something", His Essence, as His own goodness. In the future the two will be one, "like two opposites borne together", because He can do anything (line 40). And the hiding, when it shines, will have "an advantage over the light itself" (line 40). Now the heart. There are two wills in a person. One is the simple will of the soul's essence, the "hidden love" that needs no reason and will give itself for God. The other is the will born of thought: I understand, so I love. That one has limits; take away the reason and it is gone (line 41). "Unite our hearts" asks that the outer love be lifted until it too is above reason. The text admits this seems impossible: one rises from below, the other comes down from above, like fire and water (line 41). Bring it home. Think of one thing you love God for, a reason you can say. Then ask what is under that reason, what would still be there if the reason went away. The picture is the Song's "You are beautiful, my love, as Tirzah", read as two wills pleased together (line 41). It breaks because two people who agree stay two. Here the two wills are meant to become one.

**From inside.** A settling from the head into something older. The reason stays, but it stops carrying the whole weight. Some find a love they did not know they had.

**At the edge.** It touches it: the love of the soul's essence is "above reason", and the knowing that comes from His Essence is "the true something". Both are above what the mind can argue for.

**Into the day.** Tell God today one reason you love Him, then add: "and without the reason too."

**Ladder.** L07 (Know it today, bring it to the heart)

## Petach HaShaar §13: the will to live, and R. Akiva's long "echad" (lines 42-44)

**The movement.** It takes the mind below its own reasons, to the plain will to live, where love and fear are not yet two. It uses that as a window onto God's own qualities in His Essence, and onto why any Jew can give his life.

> כמו שאין להבדיל ולחלק במה שרוצ' לחיות ואינו רוצ' למו' דהיינו הך ממש
> *Just as one cannot divide between wanting to live and not wanting to die: they are one and the same.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the love that comes from understanding comes in measure, with a right and a left, joy and bitterness each in proportion to what was grasped (line 42). The will of the soul's essence also has a right and a left, love for what you love as yourself and hatred for what threatens your life. But they have no measure, and they are not two (lines 42-43). Step by step: wanting to live and not wanting to die are one wanting. That is the text's picture for God's qualities as they are in His Essence: "long days", kindness and severity not divided, "without any bound" (line 43). So "drawing out echad" is not only letting the world melt into wisdom. It is reaching the unity of His own qualities in His Essence, far above the unity of the Infinite with the ten sefirot (line 43). And every blessing of the Amidah, even for a good year and a living, is drawn from there (line 43). The root in the soul: Israel "rose in thought" in His very Essence, before any thought to create. "For this reason even the least of Israel can give his life for the sanctification of God's Name" (line 44). R. Akiva drew out "echad" "until his soul went out and was included in the simple One" (line 44). Bring it home. You do not decide each morning whether to want to live. It is under all your decisions. Sit with that plain wanting and ask where it comes from.

**From inside.** A floor opens under the reasons. The want to live, which usually feels like a private animal thing, starts to feel like a thread from somewhere very high. Some feel a quiet courage.

**At the edge.** Yes. The text goes past wisdom to "the ten hidden sefirot in the Essence" and to the soul's root "in His very Essence" (lines 43-44). What is there can be given oneself to, not grasped.

**Into the day.** When you wake tomorrow, before anything else, say "I want to live, for You."

## Petach HaShaar §14: the hidden heart, and the days that are counted (lines 45-47)

**The movement.** It shows a person two hearts in one chest and names the whole work of life: to bring the hidden one out into the light. Then it looks at a person's numbered days and finds, under them, days without number.

> אך עיקר העבודה בזה העולם אינו אלא להוציא בחי' תעלומות הלב לבא לידי גילוי אור דוקא
> *But the main work in this world is only to bring out the hidden places of the heart, so that they come to revealed light.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "with all your heart" means both inclinations, both hearts (line 45). In the right side of the heart lives the soul's hidden love, "natural and essential", too large for the mind and heart to hold (line 46). In the left lives the natural heat of the body, the animal soul. Step by step: the hidden love shows only at moments. At a real test, when even a simple Jew gives his life. On Shabbat and festivals "a tiny edge" of it. On Yom Kippur, when all turn back (line 46). The rest of the time it is like "a man hidden under the shadow of a stone": in the dark, but still shielding you from wrong through the whole day (line 46). The text's rule: love that begins as a response to thought grows, in one who seeks God in truth, into a love the mind and heart cannot hold, "with all your might" (line 46). Adam's soul was put in a body for this: "going down for the sake of going up" (line 46). Then the days. Each person is given a measured number of days, "and not one among them" is wholly lit (line 47). Yet from the soul's root in the Essence comes something without measure: "I will fill the number of your days" (line 47). Bring it home. You have had moments when something in you went further than your reasons. Remember one. That was the hidden heart, for a moment out from under the stone.

**From inside.** Recognition. "That was me, and I did not know it." Then a gentle wish for it to come out more often, not by force but by being let.

**At the edge.** It points there: the hidden love is from the soul's essence, and the days without measure come from "the eternity of the Infinite". The mind can name them; only the heart, when it opens, touches them.

**Into the day.** Do one small good act today that you have no good reason for, only because you want Him.

**Ladder.** L01 (Under everything, the spark)

## Petach HaShaar §15: the two servants (lines 48-50)

**The movement.** It gives the mind two pictures of serving God, the humble servant who cleans and the gifted craftsman who makes his master rich. It lets a person see that both are needed, and that the second completes "the Perfection of all".

> שאף על פי שהוא י"ת שלימותא דכולא מ"מ בחר בעמו ישראל שיהי' שלימותו ע"י תו"מ
> *Although He, blessed be He, is the perfection of everything, still He chose His people Israel so that His perfection would come through Torah and mitzvot.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the blessing before the Shema asks "unite our hearts to love and to fear", and the text finds both parts of the Shema in it: love in the first paragraph, fear and the yoke of mitzvot in the second (lines 48-49). Step by step, the picture. One servant sweeps the house, cleans the dishes, chops wood, draws water. He does lowly work, in fear, and hopes for his wage (line 49). That is turning from evil, giving charity beyond your strength, forcing yourself to study, and sorting the good from the fallen (line 50). The other servant is a master craftsman: he weaves, sets gems, carves gold and stone, trades in the market, like Joseph in Potiphar's house or Eliezer in Abraham's. Through him the master gains a wealth he could not have had himself. No wage is enough for him; the master loves him "as himself", more than a son (line 49). That is the doing of mitzvot with joy, "the very paths of Havayah" (line 50). When you lay tefillin, He lays tefillin. And even the craftsman's joy has trembling in it: "rejoice with trembling" (line 50). Bring it to your hands. Look at what you did yesterday. Some of it was sweeping: holding back, pushing through. Some of it was craft: a mitzvah done well and gladly. Both were for Him. The second, the text says, adds to Him something He chose to receive from you.

**From inside.** A dignity in ordinary service. Then something warmer: the sense of being wanted, not just used. Some feel a quiet pride that is not self-importance, like a craftsman in his master's house.

**At the edge.** It touches the edge in a paradox it leaves open: He is "the perfection of everything", and still He chose to be completed by Israel. The mind cannot square it and does not need to.

**Into the day.** Do one mitzvah today slowly and well, as a craftsman would, and offer it as a gift.

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Petach HaShaar §16: "to Him, not to His qualities", and the Shema before the Amidah (lines 51-54)

**The movement.** It opens two opposite doors to closeness, climbing up to lose yourself in Him and walking down in His ways, and shows that the first door is what opens the second. The Shema gives itself away; the Amidah receives.

> דכל העלאה גורם המשכה לפי ערך ההעלאה ממש
> *For every rising causes a drawing down, in exact proportion to the rising.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: two sayings seem to clash. "To Him, and not to His qualities". And "Can one cleave to the Shechinah? Rather cleave to His qualities" (lines 51-52). The verses of cleaving, "and to Him you shall cleave", mean Him Himself (line 52). Step by step: both are true, because there are two ways of cleaving. Going up, the soul gives itself away and is included, and that goes only to Him, "as with self-giving: to Him alone" (line 52). Coming down, one walks in His ways, merciful as He is merciful; that is cleaving to His qualities, and even there the aim is the Infinite clothed in them (line 52). The first is the Shema. The second is the Amidah. They are "two real opposites", and yet one hangs on the other (line 53). Without the Shema's giving-away, the eighteen blessings bring nothing down (line 53). So the Shema is "eternal life", and prayer is "life of the hour" (line 54). Bring it home. When you say "echad", do not ask for anything. Only give. Then in the Amidah, ask plainly, for health, for a living, for your children. The asking rests on the giving. The text's own picture is a person calling a friend to come to him, "near to all who call on Him". It breaks at the top: you cannot call Him down unless you first go up to Him with nothing.

**From inside.** Two motions that feel opposite: an emptying, then a receiving. People often notice that the asking afterward is calmer and more honest.

**At the edge.** Yes. The rising goes to "His very Essence", above the ten sefirot, where one cannot "resemble" Him at all, only be included (line 52). The mind does not climb there; the soul gives itself there.

**Into the day.** Before the Amidah today, pause after the Shema and say inwardly: "I have given; now I ask."

## Petach HaShaar §17: a Name for each blessing, and the king's officers (lines 55-57)

**The movement.** It teaches the mind to ask precisely, each request to its own door. Then it takes the precision away from the doors and turns it to the One behind them.

> ולא בספירה אור החכמה עצמה תליא מילתא אלא דוקא בבחי' אא"ס שמלובש במדה וספירה זו דחכמה
> *The matter does not hang on the sefirah, the light of wisdom itself, but only on the Infinite who is clothed in this quality and sefirah of wisdom.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the four letters of God's Name are a path: a contraction, a spreading, a line down, and a receiving (line 55). In the Amidah the Name in each blessing carries its own vowel. Wisdom takes a patach, an open mouth; repentance a tzeirei; forgiveness a segol; healing a cholam (lines 55-56). Step by step, the picture: a king gives each officer a written authority over one thing. One who wants bread and goes to the officer over clothing will get nothing (line 57). So each request should go to the quality it belongs to: healing to one, wisdom to another. Then the turn. The door is not the point. What gives is the Infinite light clothed in that quality, "and not the sefirah itself" (line 56). Every Name has an outside, a vessel, and an inside, His own Name as it is in Himself. Bring it home. Think of one thing you need today. Name it exactly. Then remember who you are asking: not a department, but Him, who has chosen to give through this door. The officers picture breaks, and the next section breaks it openly: He is not bound to His officers. He can send kindness through severity.

**From inside.** A sharpening of prayer. Asks become clean and specific. Under them, a steady sense that the giving comes from one Face.

**At the edge.** It points there: the "inside" of each Name is His Essence, above the ten sefirot. The mind can know the doors; it cannot know what is behind them.

**Into the day.** In one blessing of the Amidah today, name your need in one exact word before you say it.

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Petach HaShaar §18: why they cry and are not answered; the prayer of every mouth (lines 58-61)

**The movement.** It takes the fear out of not knowing the right intentions. It sends the heart past all the Names to His own Name, and finds there the One who hears "the prayer of every mouth". Then it sets the body's bowing beside the soul's.

> שומע תפלת כל פה אעפ"י שאינו יודע לכוין בשמות כלל
> *He hears the prayer of every mouth, even one who does not know how to intend the Names at all.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the Sages ask why Israel cries out and is not answered, and answer: because they do not call "with the Name" (line 58). Singular. Not the many names, but His own Name, the "Havayah echad" of the Shema, source of all the others (line 59). Step by step: prayer in the Talmud is called "mercy". Chana was "bitter of soul", and she did not aim at a sefirah; she poured out her soul (line 59). That is the root of all prayer: to call out to Him Himself, "for the gates of mercy are never locked" (line 59). So why the precise Names? For the drawing-down, so the blessing reaches its place (line 60). But He is all-capable and can send kindness through severity and severity through kindness, the way He sent a destroying voice against Sennacherib and a judging angel to do mercy (line 60). Then the body. In the Shema one sits; the bitul is inner, the point of the soul. In the Amidah one stands, bows and bends, like a servant before a king, because the Amidah asks for bodily needs (line 61). The outer bowing only works through the inner one. R. Akiva would begin prayer in one corner and be found in another, so much had he bowed (line 61). Bring it home. If you do not know any kavanot, you are not shut out. Say the words to Him, with a bitter or plain heart, and mean Him.

**From inside.** Relief. The gate was never locked. Then a simple directness, like a child talking to a parent, and a willingness to bend the body.

**At the edge.** It points to His "Name in His Essence", above all the particular Names (line 59). The mind cannot aim there with technique; only a heart poured out can.

**Into the day.** In today's prayer, bow fully at "Baruch" once, and in your own words say "I am calling You."

## Petach HaShaar §19: the Shema's new light, and a new east each day (lines 62-65)

**The movement.** It shows that the world runs on an old measure unless something new is asked for, and that only the Shema's self-giving brings a truly new light. Then it brings the newness down to the directions of space around you.

> וא"כ בכל יום מתחדש מזרח חדש שלא היה אתמול
> *So each day a new east is renewed, one that was not there yesterday.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the Shema and the Amidah point opposite ways. In the Shema one gives oneself to the Essence, where self-giving belongs, above reason; in the Amidah one draws down wisdom, healing, a living (line 62). Yet the Amidah draws from the Shema. Step by step: the unions above go on always, but they only "renew the old", keeping the worlds alive by the first measure (line 63). When the world needs more than its measure, a sick man to be healed, a poor man whose supply was cut off, a new light must come "not according to the first measure". That comes only through the Shema (lines 63-64). And in the Shema the light that comes down first melts everything into the Essence. From there peace comes even between opposites: "He makes peace between Michael and Gabriel", fire and water that do not put each other out (line 63). Then the Sages' words, "make Him king in heaven and earth and the four directions", turn out to be exact. Each day the directions of the physical world are made again from their root, "a new east that was not there yesterday" (line 64). The whole unity hangs on the lowest, physical directions: when the lowest rises, everything rises with it (line 64). Bring it home. Stand at your window in the morning. The east you face is not yesterday's east. It was just said into being, and your Shema is part of what lets something new come through it.

**From inside.** Freshness. The room feels less worn. Along with it, a sense of responsibility: what I say this morning may open something the world does not yet have.

**At the edge.** It points there: the new light comes "from His very Essence", which is "set apart even from being a source of sources" (line 62). The mind can see the newness; it cannot see where it comes from.

**Into the day.** Tomorrow morning, face east for a moment and say: "New, today, from You."

## Petach HaShaar §20: "Lift up your eyes and see who created these" (lines 66-68)

**The movement.** It aims the eyes at the counted, bounded world and asks them to find the Infinite exactly there, in the counting. The mind is shown that only the unlimited can make a limit this exact.

> אלא צ"ל דהוא י"ת כל יכול וכמו שמכחו נמשך בבלתי גבול כך הוא מוציא במספר וגבול דוקא
> *Rather, He, blessed be He, is all-capable: just as from His power comes the unbounded, so He brings out in number and bound.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Baruch Shem" is the lower unity, the kingship of God hidden in the lower worlds, the sons of Jacob's answer, said quietly now and aloud in the future (line 66). And the prophet says: "Lift your eyes on high and see who created these; He brings out their host by number" (line 66). Step by step: everyone knows God created the world. The prophet asks for more: to see the Infinite in what is already made and bounded, "because the action resembles the Actor" (line 66). Number and limit are not a failure of the Infinite. "These are His mighty acts" (line 68): the same power that is without end brings things out by number. The text's pictures: a spring that never stops, poured into vessels of fixed sizes; a great teacher whose wisdom has no end, with each student taking as much as his vessel holds (line 68). No two angels share one task, because each has its own letter (line 68). "Not one is missing." Bring it home. Look at the things on your table. Each has an exact size, weight and shape. Count three of them. The text says the exactness itself is His doing, and so is the fact that they keep coming, day after day, without a break. The spring picture breaks where the text says it does: a spring is fed from a deep, not from itself. Here the source and the one who measures are the same.

**From inside.** A new respect for small exact things. The world stops looking random and starts looking counted, each thing wanted at its size. Some feel counted too.

**At the edge.** It goes near: the Essence dwells in the kingship "as in the higher unity" (line 66), and He is "all-capable", holding bound and unbound. The mind can see the bound; it can only believe the Infinite in it.

**Into the day.** Count something today, coins, steps, stairs, and after the last one say: "You brought these out by number."

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Petach HaShaar §21: the more bounded, the higher its root (lines 69-73)

**The movement.** It overturns the mind's habit of ranking things by how spiritual they are. The more exact, limited and named a thing is, the text says, the higher its root. The mind is sent to the smallest particulars to find the deepest source.

> וכל שבא בהגבלה יותר שרשו עליון יותר
> *Whatever comes in greater limitation, its root is higher.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Lift your eyes on high": the "on high" is the Essence, above number (line 69). And the prophet names three things: that He created these; that He brings them out by number; and, the greatest wonder, that "He calls each one by name" (line 71). Step by step, the picture is a mind at work. Ideas flow without end, and at once letters and words form around them, limited, counted. Yet the power to make letters, to limit, comes from a higher root than the power to shine (line 70). A parable is lower than its meaning, and still "the parable of the Ancient One", the revealed Torah, is rooted higher than its light (line 70). The tablets' engraved letters are higher than the spoken Ten Commandments (lines 70-71). The root: the letters of "Let there be a firmament", bounded into each creature, are higher at their root than the first light of wisdom (line 72). That is why "Baruch Shem", the lower unity, has an advantage over the higher (line 72). And each angel is called by the name of its one task, a blade of grass, a fire; the One who calls each by name is the Essence at the very end, "their beginning wedged in their end" (lines 71-72). Bring it to your own life. The very exact things, your one name, your own small tasks, the precise words of a blessing, are not further from Him than lofty feelings. The text says they reach higher. The picture of letters breaks: our letters limit our ideas because we are small. His limiting is His strength, "these are His mighty acts".

**From inside.** The large, vague spiritual longing narrows into something you can hold. Often a kind of affection for the exact: for the law, for names, for the small task done right.

**At the edge.** It points to it: the power to limit comes from "the Essence of the first wisdom", above the higher unity (line 70). The mind reaches the particular; the root of the particular is out of its sight.

**Into the day.** Say one blessing today with every word exact, as if each letter were engraved.

## Petach HaShaar §22: "sick with love", and the palace of the law (lines 74-77)

**The movement.** It names a pain many seekers know, a longing too big for the body to hold, and does not shame it. Then it gives the longing a home: the letters of the Torah, where the Holy One sits and studies across from you in one room.

> אבל ד"א של הלכה שנק' היכלא עלאה הוא מקומו והיכלו הפנימי העצמי
> *But the four cubits of halacha, called "the higher palace", are His place and His inner, essential palace.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: in exile Israel says "sustain me with raisin cakes, for I am sick with love" (line 74). The text's picture: a sick man craves food more than a healthy one, but his body cannot keep it down. A sicker man has lost all appetite; that one is in danger (line 74). So a soul that longs and cannot hold its longing is not lost. It is sick with love. Step by step, the reason: now we know only that He is, from His works, not what He is. "Knowledge of existence, not of essence" (lines 74-75). Even souls in Gan Eden and the angels see only His radiance, and pine. In the future His Essence will be seen, down to this world, and bodies will live from it (line 75). Until then, the Essence is reached through Torah. Its wisdom did not fall in the shattering; it "does not hide His Essence at all, but is a vessel to hold His very Essence" (line 76). So after the longing, the soul is comforted, and comes to joy in study and mitzvot (line 76). The picture: two people sitting in one palace, the palace around them on every side. When you study, "the Holy One sits and studies opposite him", and you are "brothers and friends" (line 77). Heaven is His throne, but the four cubits of halacha are His room. Bring it home. Where do you study, even for ten minutes? That small space, the text says, is where He lives, not visits. The picture breaks only in this: in a palace the walls are stone. Here the walls are letters.

**From inside.** First, permission to long without being ashamed of it. Then comfort, and a warmth, like sitting across a table from someone who wants to be with you.

**At the edge.** It points there: knowing His essence belongs to the future, and even now the Torah is a vessel for His Essence. The mind studies the words; what sits across from it is beyond it.

**Into the day.** Learn one halacha today, slowly, and picture the Holy One learning it with you.

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Petach HaShaar §23: the vessel, the love for His coming down, and thought, speech and deed (lines 78-82)

**The movement.** It turns the direction of love around. Love that only wants to rise out of the world is not yet the Shema's love. The love it asks for wants Him to come down, into vessels, into mitzvot, into this world.

> והוא שיחפוץ וישתוקק בהמשכת גלוי אא"ס למטה
> *That he should want and long for the revelation of the Infinite to be drawn down below.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the higher unity is the Shema's first verse; the lower unity, "Baruch Shem", is light drawn into vessels (line 78). The inner vessels are the mitzvot, and Israel is "the vessel her husband uses" (line 78). "This is the main thing: that the Holy One desired a dwelling in the lower worlds, and not in the higher" (line 78). Step by step: nothing, however high, can hold Him. "The heavens cannot contain You." But in the 248 commandments, come down into physical deeds, His very Essence dwells, because that was the first intention (line 79). Without the vessel of the mitzvah, the Infinite would not be revealed even in the highest worlds (line 79). So the love must match. A love that only runs up, wanting to melt away, draws Him up, not down. What draws a new light down is a love shaped like the light: wanting Him revealed here, in the mind and heart, in study and in charity (line 79). "Every rising must be in the likeness of the drawing down" (line 79). Then the order of the Shema's first paragraph: hearing and love (thought), "speak of them" (speech), "bind them" (deed). Speech is the go-between; even a child reciting a verse without understanding holds up the world (lines 80-81). And the outermost vessel, the deed, "rises above all" (line 82). Bring it home. When you pray, what do you want? To get away, or for Him to come here? Try wanting the second: Him in your kitchen, your work, your hands.

**From inside.** A turning of the heart's direction, from escape to welcome. People often feel their ordinary life become the place where love is aimed, not the place it is aimed away from.

**At the edge.** It says what lies above the reach of any light: "there is no comparison at all" between the emanated and the inner Essence (line 78). Only the vessel of the deed catches what no light can.

**Into the day.** Put on tefillin, or put a coin in a charity box, and say: "Come down here."

## Petach HaShaar §24: "From my flesh I see God"; knowing and believing (lines 83-85)

**The movement.** It moves God's life in the world out of the column of "things I believe" and into the column of "things I see", the way you see that your own body is alive. Faith is then kept for what is truly above: the part of Him that the world does not touch.

> וא"כ כמו שירגיש בחיי נפשו הוא יכול להרגיש עד"ז בחיי החיים כולם
> *So just as he can feel the life of his own soul, he can feel in the same way the Life of all life.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Know the God of your father", and "they believed in God" (line 83). Knowing and believing seem to be opposites. The text says: most people call it faith to believe that God gives life to all worlds. That is not faith at all. That is something seen (line 83). Step by step, the picture: no one has ever seen the spirit that keeps his body alive. Yet he is sure of it, because flesh by itself is only dust. The mind's sight is as good as the eye's. "From my flesh I see God" (line 83). The whole world is like one body, and His life in it like one soul (line 84). And knowing, da'at, means staying with it, deep, until you feel it; the warning is only "do not forget" (line 84). Then what faith is for. There is a part of the soul that does not go into the body. It is not hurt by the body's pain or lifted by its pleasure; it surrounds "head and foot alike" (lines 84-85). So there is a kingship of God that does not go into the worlds at all. For that, He says, "I, God, have not changed": nothing is different before and after creation (line 85). That is believed, not seen. Bring it home. Move your fingers. You do not believe they move by the life in you; you know it. Now look at the room, the trees outside, the people: the same kind of knowing can hold that they live by His life. The picture breaks, as the text marks: the soul is affected by the body it enlivens; He is not. And the soul-in-body picture does not reach His surrounding kingship at all.

**From inside.** A calm certainty, not a leap. Things look inhabited. Alongside it, a humbler sense of something above what you can see, which you trust rather than grasp.

**At the edge.** Yes. Sovev, His essential kingship, "above any grasp", unchanged by the world (lines 84-85). It is reached now only by faith, which the book will insist must still be drawn toward knowing.

**Into the day.** Once today, look at your own hand and say: "Your life is in this, as mine is."

**Ladder.** L07 (Know it today, bring it to the heart)

## Petach HaShaar §25: a land flowing with milk and honey (lines 86-87)

**The movement.** It shows that a love which only sits in you does not grow. What makes it grow, like milk in a child, is contemplating the part of God that is above the world. The mind is asked to nurse on something it cannot hold.

> אבל ע"י ההתבוננות בבחי' סובב יגדלו המדות ויתרחב ויתוסף אור האהבה
> *But through contemplating the "surrounding" level, the heart's qualities grow, and the light of love widens and increases.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "The earth shall be full of knowledge of God": the earth is faith, the last of all levels, and the first is wedged in it (line 86). What now comes only as faith will one day be known and felt, "and all flesh shall see" (line 86). The Shema already begins it: His surrounding Name becomes "our God", and then "One" on the earth, "because the main drawing-down of sovev must be on the earth" (line 86). Step by step, the picture: a nursing child. The milk makes his limbs grow little by little, more than they would on their own (line 87). The hidden love every Jew has "is very small, and does not grow or increase at any time; it stays as it was planted" (line 87). Contemplating what is above the world is the milk. And the honey is delight. What is always in front of your eyes does not delight you much. Delight comes from "a thing wondrous beyond grasp" (line 86). So "then you will delight in God" means in what surrounds, not in what fills (line 86). Bring it home. If your love for God feels the same size it was years ago, the text says that is how the inborn love is. Give it something wondrous to think about, slowly, day after day. The picture breaks in one place: a child grows up and is weaned. Here the nursing is meant to go on until the earth itself is full of knowing.

**From inside.** A slow widening in the chest over days, not in one sitting. Sweetness, and a new appetite for the higher thoughts. The text calls it "restoring the soul".

**At the edge.** It rests there: sovev is "above any grasp"; its delight is in its being beyond. The mind drinks what it cannot contain.

**Into the day.** Spend two minutes today on one thought that is too big for you, and do not try to finish it.

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Petach HaShaar §26: "Know today", and there is no other (lines 88-90)

**The movement.** It sets the work in this world and in this day. The surrounding light that is now only believed is drawn into knowing through the mitzvot. And "today" is the one time a person can still turn, because the surrounding light rests here.

> וע"כ כמו בשמים ממעל אין עוד כך על הארץ מתחת אין עוד
> *So just as in the heavens above there is no other, so on the earth below there is no other.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: in the future the earth will be full of knowing of sovev, "real knowing and feeling", not just a glimmer (line 88). How can the light above the vessel come into a vessel? Only by raising the vessel, through practical mitzvot: the "do nots" burn out the evil, the "dos" draw the high lights down until a person is "adam", like the One above (lines 88-89). Step by step: "Today to do them, tomorrow to receive their reward." Faith has to be fed, like a flock by a shepherd, and it is fed by the 248 positive mitzvot (line 89). Sinai showed only memale; "know today" asks that sovev too come into knowing, through Torah and deeds (line 89). Why today? The world to come is fixed, each thing in its place like limbs of a body. There, a person tied to what he did can only be shaken loose, like a man bound in a sack and flung from a sling. Here, the surrounding light rests, and teshuvah can turn darkness around from end to end (line 90). Then the root: God's word is like speech still inside the speaker's mind, not yet separate. So "just as in the heavens above there is no other, so on the earth below there is no other" (line 90). Bring it home. The earth below is your floor, your street, your day. Say "there is no other" there, not in the sky.

**From inside.** A plain seriousness about the day. Not heavy: urgent in a quiet way. And under it, the steadying truth that the ground you stand on is as full of Him as heaven.

**At the edge.** It names what will come into knowing: sovev, "I am who I am", above all the chain of worlds (line 90). Now it comes only through the deed, and the knowing is still ahead.

**Into the day.** Do one mitzvah today with the words "Today, not tomorrow."

**Ladder.** L07 (Know it today, bring it to the heart)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §1: why "Havayah" twice; the heart's two flows (lines 93-95)

**The movement.** It opens the gate with honest questions and then turns to the body. It shows a person his own heart sending one flow up to the mind and another down to the limbs. It also tells him plainly that thinking changes the heart only for a moment.

> משא"כ עצם המדו' לטוב ולרע לא ישונה טבען לעולם
> *But the heart's qualities in themselves, for good or for bad, never change their nature.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: why does the verse say "Havayah" twice, and why "our God", and why is the ayin of "Shema" large (line 93)? And why command "choose life" at all, when no one would choose death? The answers need a long road, and the road begins in the chest. Step by step, the text's picture: the heart has an inner side and an outer side. From the inner side a fine breath rises, becoming voice in the throat and feeling in the mind: love, anger, pity (lines 93-94). From the outer side the blood goes down, feeding the limbs, so the hand can work and the foot can walk. On a fast day the limbs grow weak because that flow slows (line 95). Then the plain truth about feelings. When the heart is stirred, the mind fills with it. But the feelings in the mind change "like a garment": turn your thought away for a moment, and they are gone. The heart's own nature, for good or for bad, does not change so easily (line 95). The text notes that the mind also rules the heart, and keeps both (line 95). Bring it home. Remember the last time an idea moved you in prayer and was gone an hour later. That was the mind's copy of a feeling. The heart's own grain is slower, and deeper. The picture is anatomy. It is a map, and it breaks, as maps do: a heart is flesh. The text uses it only as a mirror for "the Adam above".

**From inside.** A sobering, not a discouragement. Less surprise when a feeling fades. More respect for the slow work of changing what the heart is.

**At the edge.** It does not reach the edge here. This section is groundwork for the firmament and the two unities.

**Into the day.** When a feeling for God comes today, do one small deed with it before it fades.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §2: the firmament, the sieve, and the screen over the living creatures (lines 96-99)

**The movement.** It asks how one heart can send out two opposite flows, and answers with a divider made of both sides. The mind learns to see the screen between high and low as a kindness that lets each be itself.

> הגמשת אור זיו השכינה שלא יתבטלו במציאותם לגמרי
> *A coarsening of the radiance of the Shechinah, so that they are not wholly dissolved out of existence.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Let there be a firmament in the midst of the waters, and let it divide" (line 93). In the body, the diaphragm separates the organs of breath from the organs of food; in the heart, it lets the fine rise to the mind and the coarse go down to the limbs (line 97). Step by step, the picture is a sieve. Before the sieve, fine and coarse are mixed, and neither is fully itself. The sieve must be of a middle thickness: too fine or too coarse, and it separates nothing (line 97). So the firmament must be made of both, "the utmost of the spiritual come into the utmost of the physical" (line 97). The same with a parable. A good parable is a screen over a deep idea: through it the student learns, and without it he could not tell the inside of the idea from its outside (line 97). Over the heads of the holy creatures is "a firmament like ice": the radiance of the Shechinah made coarser, "so that they are not dissolved out of existence entirely" (line 98). Bring it home. Think of the things that screen God from you: your body, your work, your ordinary day. The text says a screen can be a mercy. Without it you could not stand. The picture of the sieve breaks in the end: the text foretells a time when "waters meet waters", the upper and lower no longer divided (line 98).

**From inside.** A softening toward the veils in your life. The distance feels less like punishment and more like care. Some feel gratitude for being allowed to exist at all.

**At the edge.** It points there: without the screen the creatures would be "dissolved out of existence", which is what full revelation would do. The mind can think this; it cannot bear it, which is the point.

**Into the day.** When something ordinary gets between you and a spiritual moment today, say: "This too is a kindness."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §3: the heart above, three Shabbat rests, and a divide made for the lowest (lines 100-103)

**The movement.** It lifts the picture of the heart up to "the Adam above" and then shows why there is a divide at all: because the first, simple will wanted the lowest creation. The mind is asked to see distance as something wanted for the sake of the bottom.

> ומפני שסוף מעשה עלה ורצון הפשוט בתחלה שיהי' דוקא תכלית שפלות הבריאה
> *And because the end of the deed rose first, and the simple will at the beginning was that there be precisely the utmost lowliness of creation.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: above, as in a person, there are feelings, thoughts and speech. The worlds come from His thought and speech, and are called "worlds" only because those are garments; before the feelings themselves "they count as nothing" (line 101). Step by step, Shabbat has three rests. Friday night, the ten sayings return to the heart they came from. Shabbat day, the feelings rise into one thought. At the third, "vayinafash", it comes "to His very Essence" (line 101). So each week the many are gathered back to the one. Then the divide: why separate upper waters from lower at all? Because what was wanted first was the very bottom, "the utmost lowliness of creation", and that could not exist without a divide (line 102). By the divide, the lower goes lower than it could have, and the upper rises higher than it was. The text's picture is the pillar of cloud between Israel and Egypt, "made of two opposites, darkness and light": dark for one side, light for the other (line 103). Bring it home. The place you live, far from any Temple, crowded with plain needs, was not an accident at the end of the line. The text says it was wanted first. The picture breaks where pictures of walls always break: this wall was built for the sake of what is on the far side of it.

**From inside.** A strange tenderness toward the ordinary and even the low. The sense that God is not stuck with this world but chose it. On Shabbat, a gathering-in.

**At the edge.** It points to the Shabbat's third rest, "His very Essence" in the inner Keter, where "no thought grasps Him" (line 101). The text only names it.

**Into the day.** This Shabbat, in the afternoon, sit for a minute and let the week's many things gather into one.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §4: "Choose life"; the pleasure hidden in water; eating for the soul (lines 104-106)

**The movement.** It takes the mind to the table. Pleasure itself has a root in God, and the same bread can make a person coarse or can lift its good upward. The choice of life is made in a mouthful.

> והטוב שבהן עולה במעלות עונג העליון שמוסיף חכמ' העליונה האלקית
> *And the good in them rises through the levels of the higher delight, and adds to the higher, divine wisdom.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Choose life" has two meanings, and both are true: choose good over evil in the mixed world, and choose the Tree of Life above the mixture altogether (line 104). Step by step, the picture is water. Every sweetness in fruit grows from water; water makes things spread and multiply, which is what pleasure does. So hidden in water is a root of delight (lines 104-105). The upper waters hold delight with no evil in it. The lower waters hold delight mixed with good and bad. Then the table. Eat coarsely, from craving, and the bad in the pleasure comes out as anger, lust, violence. Eat to satisfy the soul, and the good in the food "rises through the levels of the higher delight" (line 105). The stomach sorts food, pushing out the waste and sending the best to heart and mind; the text reads that as a map of the angels sorting delight above (line 106). In the end there will be nothing to sort: "even the pig will be purified", because everything will have been refined (line 106). Bring it to your own plate tonight. Before you eat, name the good in it: its taste, its strength for your day. Then eat as someone lifting that good, not only taking it.

**From inside.** Slower eating. A small freedom from the pull of appetite. Some feel, for the first time, that a meal can be prayer without any words.

**At the edge.** It points to it: the delight in the upper waters is "His hidden delight", and at Shabbat "the pleasures of Israel" rise to His Essence. The text does not stay there here.

**Into the day.** At one meal today, say a blessing slowly and eat the first bite for the soul.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §5: good and bad even in the mind; not by bread alone (lines 107-110)

**The movement.** It stays honest about the soul: even the fine inner self carries good and bad natures. Then it names what feeds the soul beneath the bread of study: "every word from God's mouth" inside it.

> רק שלא על הלחם לבדו עיקר כ"א על כל מוצא פי ה' שבתורה יחיה האדם ע"י הלחם
> *Only that the bread alone is not the main thing; rather, by every word from God's mouth in the Torah does a person live, through the bread.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: we might think the heart's inner side, rising to the mind, is all good. It is not. Some minds lean by nature to harsh judgment, some to kindness; food only strengthens what is already there (lines 107-108). The whole soul comes from the mixed tree. Step by step: above, in "the Adam of Atzilut", the upper half is all good, the Tree of Life (line 108). Below, delight mixed with evil is sorted, and the good rises and adds a new delight above, "a pleasing scent" (line 108). The days of Moshiach will have felt pleasure, all good, like "the bread of the Presence"; the world to come will have no eating at all, only His own delight (line 108). Torah now is "bread": it sorts the pure from the impure, the fit from the unfit (lines 109-110). On Shabbat even that sorting pauses, "because in truth all is one in the Infinite" (line 109). Then the verse: "Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word from God's mouth." The text does not say the bread of Torah is nothing. It says the life in it is the voice inside it, His own delight spoken (line 110). Bring it home. When you study a law, you are eating bread. Stop once and listen for the Speaker in it. The picture breaks with Moses: "he did not eat bread", he lived from the word alone, as all will in the end (line 110).

**From inside.** A deeper hunger, and a quieter kind of fullness. Study stops being only information and becomes being spoken to.

**At the edge.** It reaches toward it: "the essential delights in His primordial wisdom", before the root of all worlds (line 110). The mind eats the bread; the life in it is beyond the mind.

**Into the day.** When you learn today, read one line aloud, and hear it as said to you now.

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §6: the large ayin; love that no deed can empty (lines 111-115)

**The movement.** It takes the mind down through four floors of a person: the deed, the feeling, the goodness that is his nature, the self. Then it uses that stair to point to God's own qualities in His Essence, and past them to Him.

> כי כל המעשה לא יגיע רק לאפס קצה אור האהבה
> *For all the doing reaches only the very edge of the light of the love.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the large ayin of "Shema" stands for God's qualities in themselves, before they go out into thought and speech (lines 111-112). "With You is the source of life": there is a source, and a source of the source (line 111). Step by step, the picture is love for a friend. The love itself is simple and without parts. It comes out in many kindnesses, in words and in thoughts, and "all the doing reaches only the edge" of it (line 112). Under the feeling is something deeper: a man who is good by nature, "good in essence", before he wants anything. His will and his thinking lean to kindness because of what he is (line 113). So there are four levels: the deed; the feeling in heart and mind; the goodness set in the soul's essence, the yechidah, with no measure either in itself or in its spreading; and the soul itself (lines 113-114). Above, the same: the worlds, the lights of Atzilut, the ten hidden sefirot in His Essence, and "the Emanator Himself, not of any of these qualities at all" (line 114). And the reason for everything is a "desire for kindness" in His Essence, "as the good wants to do good" (line 115). Bring it home. Think of someone you love. List what you have done for them. Feel how the list does not reach the love. Then go under the love to what you are. The picture holds a long way, and breaks only at the last floor, where He is not "good" as a quality at all.

**From inside.** A tenderness toward your own deeds: they are true and still only the edge. A settling into something deeper than feelings, which does not rise and fall.

**At the edge.** Yes. It names "His very Essence, not of any of these qualities, not even hidden" (line 114). The soul's yechidah is the picture for it, and even the soul's own essential qualities are "nullified" before the soul itself.

**Into the day.** Do one kindness today and afterward say inwardly: "This is only the edge."
