## Shaar Kriat Shema §7: the large dalet; the end of thought, where the beginning is fixed (lines 116-119)

**The movement.** It shows the mind a thought that is not chosen, a quiet thinking that hovers around a good person like a surrounding cloud. It uses that to picture God's own "I will reign", where the end of His thought holds its beginning.

> והוא מה שאא"ס למעלה מעלה עד אין שיעור ע"כ מתפשט למטה עד אין תכלית
> *That is: because the Infinite is high above without measure, therefore He spreads down without end.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the large dalet of "echad" is thought, just as the large ayin was feeling (line 116). In us, feelings in the mind at once clothe themselves in thoughts, turning the thing over in many ways (line 116). Step by step: there is also a thought that comes from the good in your nature, not from any choice. It "hovers over a person, surrounding the mind and even the will". He thinks it "without choosing", a simple garment for what he is (line 116). Above, the same: His "understanding without number" (line 117). The root: "I will reign" is the end of His thought, and in it the first will is wedged (line 117). Everything between, wisdom and feelings, is a passageway. And the rule: because He is above without measure, He reaches below without end, down to the kingship of this world (line 117). So "Baruch Shem" is His own kingship, coming down all the way (line 117). And the dalet clothes only the last third of the heart's qualities, where a decision turns into giving, because the beginning of the will is complete only when it arrives (line 118). Bring it home. Notice the thoughts that come to you without your choosing, the ones that show what you are. The text says God, too, has an end in mind that holds His beginning, and you are in it. The picture breaks: your unchosen thought is small and passing; His is the root of all worlds.

**From inside.** A widening that feels like being held. The sense that the far top and the near bottom are not apart. Some notice their own quiet goodness with less suspicion.

**At the edge.** It points to "the beginning of the simple will in His Essence", which the end holds (line 117). The mind stands at the end and cannot see the beginning, only that it is there.

**Into the day.** When you say the dalet of "echad" today, think: "Here, at the end, is where You began."

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §8: echad and yachid (lines 120-123)

**The movement.** It teaches the mind the difference between two words for "one". Echad is a one that can be counted, or that holds many inside it. Yachid is alone, with nothing to count it against. The mind is led to the edge of number itself.

> הנה זהו הנק' בשם יחיד כמו היחיד שאינו בגדר מספר
> *This is what is called yachid, like the single one that is not within number.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: why does the verse say "Havayah" twice? The first is yachid and the second is echad (line 120). "Echad" is the first in a count: one, then two, then three. Or it is a one that holds many, as a thousand holds its hundreds (line 120). "Yachid" has no second, and nothing hidden in it to divide: "no sharing and no joining in number at all" (line 120). Step by step, the pictures. A compound of fire and water holds both, even when you cannot see them apart. Something simple holds nothing to take apart (line 120). A father's bond to his son is simple at its core, but it can come out as two opposite wills, to reward him and to discipline him (lines 120-121). The same in prayer: one point of the soul that later becomes joy and weeping, loving good and hating evil (line 121). Under even that is the soul itself, before any feeling at all, like a person's love of his own self, or Jonathan's love for David, "as his own soul"; Abraham stood the test with "the whole essence of his soul" (line 121). Above: the ten sefirot hidden in His Essence are "simple unity", an echad. "You are One, but not in number", "not of any of these qualities at all, even hidden": that is yachid (line 122). Of His very self one cannot even say "He and His life are one" (line 123). Bring it home. Count what is in your room: one chair, two chairs. Then think of your own self, not your feelings, just that you are. You do not count it. Now let that point you, very far, to Him. The picture breaks, since you are not alone in the world; He is.

**From inside.** A stillness under the counting mind. Numbers fall away for a moment. It can feel like standing in a very large, quiet place.

**At the edge.** Yes, fully. Yachid is "His Essence as it is, before He measured in Himself what would be" (line 122). The mind can only say what it is not: not counted, not compound, not even "one with His life".

**Into the day.** Say "echad" once today and let the word point past itself to "alone".

## Shaar Kriat Shema §9: the two Havayahs; "because he desired Me" (lines 124-128)

**The movement.** It shows the soul giving itself into echad and finding itself given into yachid, above it. Then it turns to the promise: the One the soul desires is with it in trouble before it calls.

> אזי ממילא נמסר נפשו ביחיד שלמעלה מא'
> *Then, of itself, his soul is given over into the Yachid that is above One.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the first Havayah is yachid, "before He contracted Himself, with nothing besides Him at all"; the second is echad, His qualities in His Essence, "like a flame bound to a coal" (line 124). Drawing out "echad" means reaching days that are "long at their head as at their end", with no beginning (lines 124-125). Step by step: self-giving goes to Him Himself, "to Him and not to His qualities". So why give the soul "into echad", which is below yachid (line 126)? Because a Jew rose in His first thought and must give himself to his own root. And when he gives his whole self into echad, "then, of itself, his soul is given into the Yachid above" (line 126). "When a person's spirit goes up, it brings spirit down." Then the Psalm: "Because he desired Me, I will rescue him." "Desired" (chashak) means the whole soul drawn after one thing, until it can want nothing else. "Me" means His very self (line 127). That desire has no opposite in evil, so "I will rescue him" (line 127). The text's picture: a father who sees his son in trouble does not wait to be called. "Before they call, I will answer", "for I am with him in trouble" (line 127). And "though I walk through the valley of death, I fear no evil, for You are with me" (line 128). Bring it home. If there is one thing you want with your whole self, see whether underneath it you want Him. Give that want into "echad". The picture of the father holds; it breaks only because a father can be far away. He cannot.

**From inside.** Courage, and being accompanied. A sense that the self given away is not lost but caught. Some feel a plain safety they cannot explain.

**At the edge.** Yes. It names Yachid, "before all the ancient ones", "not in the count of ten" (line 127). The soul reaches it not by knowing but by giving itself, "of itself".

**Into the day.** In a hard moment today say: "You are with me in this, before I even call."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §10: "Hear, Israel": the one glance and its inner point (lines 129-132)

**The movement.** It asks why the verse is said to Israel, and answers that Israel is the go-between that makes the One-alone and the One-of-all the same. The mind is shown a whole plan seen at a glance, and at its center, one inner wish. That wish is you.

> מפני שישראל דוקא הוא בחי' הממוצע בין יחיד לאחד
> *Because Israel, precisely, is the intermediary between Yachid and Echad.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: after the contraction He is still alone, "there is nothing besides Him", and "I, God, have not changed" (line 129). How can the One of all things be equal to the One who is alone? "Hear, Israel": because Israel is the go-between (line 129). Step by step, the picture: a man planning a great building or a large business. In one thought he sees the whole of it, beginning and end, every detail hidden inside, "in one glance" (lines 129-130). The thought is simple, and yet holds all the parts. It surrounds the whole like a sphere around a line (line 130). And at the center of the whole plan is one inner aim: the profit, the joy of the finished house. "God saw all He had made, and behold it was very good" (line 131). That inner aim is what "Israel rose in thought" means: the pleasure the whole plan was for (line 131). So when Israel comes down to this world and completes the unions above by its Torah and mitzvot, the very thing the Essence wanted is done. "So there is no change at all between Yachid in His Essence and the unity of God in the worlds" (line 132). Bring it home. You are not a detail in someone else's plan. The text says you are the reason for the plan. The picture of the builder breaks: a builder gains something he lacked. He lacks nothing, and still chose to delight in this.

**From inside.** Being wanted, at the root. Not pride; a steadiness. The sense that your small Shema matters to the whole.

**At the edge.** It points to it: in His Essence, before any contraction, "the inner delight" already held Israel (line 131). The mind can hold the builder's glance; it cannot see into the Owner's delight.

**Into the day.** Before the Shema today, say your own name and "for this You made it all."

**Ladder.** L01 (Under everything, the spark)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §11: the contraction that hides nothing, only holds back (lines 133-136)

**The movement.** It takes away a frightening picture of the contraction, as if God had stepped out and left an empty room. What is left is quieter: not a cutting-off, only His own light not showing itself.

> שאין זה הפסק גמור רק העדר גילוי אור העצמי
> *That this is not a complete break, only the absence of the revelation of His own light.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: if nothing changed before and after the contraction, what is the contraction? And how can the simple One become many? The answer: the many were already in Him, "He and His life and His causes are one", because He can do anything, rise without end and spread down without end (lines 133-134). Step by step: the trace that remains after the contraction is His power to rise; the line is His power to come down. Both were in Him before. So "there is no change at all between before the contraction and after" (line 134). The text's picture: a person who holds one single thought. That is a great narrowing for the soul, "one thought alone is tight for the soul", much tighter than many thoughts. And the narrowing comes from the soul's own strength (line 135). Or a man who stops thinking about himself and thinks about a task outside himself: he has not left, he is only turned (line 136). So the contraction is "not a complete break, only the absence of the revelation of His own light" (line 136). Below Atzilut there is a real screen, the text adds; but here, between Yachid and Echad, "no break at all" (line 136). Bring it home. When God feels absent, the text suggests another word: turned, not gone. The picture breaks: we turn because we are limited. His turning is His power.

**From inside.** A quiet reassurance. The emptiness feels less like abandonment and more like a held breath. Some find it easier to pray into the silence.

**At the edge.** It touches it: "He knows everything by knowing Himself", and what He "measured in Himself" is beyond any comparison with what came out (line 135). The mind can follow the parable only to the door.

**Into the day.** When you feel far today, say: "You have not left; You have only not shown Yourself."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §12: "With You is the source of life": four ways a thing can come out (lines 137-140)

**The movement.** It slows the mind down to sort how one thing comes from another: unveiled from hiding, pushed out by a power, grown new, or clothed. It is careful and patient work, and it leaves the mind able to tell real making-new from mere showing.

> וסימנו שמהווה לאין שיעור ולא יכלה כוחו כלל
> *And its sign is that it brings into being without limit, and its power never runs out.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "For with You is the source of life; in Your light we see light." The first half says "with You", with no change; the second speaks of a light from a light, which is a lesser thing (line 137). How can both be true? First, the kinds of coming-out. Step by step. Some things only come out of hiding: a baby from the womb, treasure from a box, a flame from a coal, speech from thought. Nothing new is made (lines 137-138). Such sources run out: when the thought's letters end, the speech ends (line 138). Other things are truly new, like a plant from the soil or a child from a seed: "and its sign is that it brings into being without limit, and its power never runs out" (line 138). In between is a power and its act: a hand that can move any way at all. And a mind that can think: but the mind must often strain and still not find the idea, because a new insight is like a birth, "wisdom comes from nothing" (line 139). And light from the soul in the body is something else again: a "clothing" (line 140). Bring it home. Watch your hand move; it simply does. Now try to have a new idea; it does not simply come. The text uses this difference to teach how the sefirot come from Him. The pictures are good but they all break at the top: the text will say that none of them fully fits His light.

**From inside.** A clarity, like sorting tools on a workbench. For some, a new respect for the moments when something truly new comes to them.

**At the edge.** Not here. This section builds tools for the next ones.

**Into the day.** When a new thought comes to you today, say: "This came from nothing."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §13: body and garment; held, and not held (lines 141-143)

**The movement.** It asks how the soul is in the body: joined as in a limb, or worn like a coat. It finds both, and uses that doubleness to show how He is in the worlds: held by them, and not held.

> וזהו מארז"ל הוא מקומו של עולם ואין העולם מקומו הרי ב' הפכי' בנושא א'
> *And this is what the Sages said: He is the place of the world and the world is not His place: two opposites in one subject.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: a body and a garment are different. The soul moves the hand just by wanting; it cannot move a coat by wanting. It feels the body's pain; it does not feel a tear in the coat (line 141). So the soul "spreads" into the body. And yet a blind man can father a sighted child: the soul itself lost nothing when the eye failed. So it is also "clothed" (line 141). Step by step: a garment has one advantage. Whoever wears it is wholly inside it, and must take it off to leave. A person in a house cannot be in another house (line 141). Elijah was not at the great gathering of the Zohar because at that hour he was in a body, saving two people (line 141). Above: "He grasps all worlds, and none grasps Him" (line 142). He is in the worlds as in a garment, wholly. But no one holds Him, and He never has to "take it off". "He is the place of the world and the world is not His place: two opposites in one subject" (line 143). Bring it home. Think of your own body: you are in it and more than it. Now think of your room: He fills it and is not caught by it. The picture of the garment breaks at the end, as the text says: He needs no undressing to be elsewhere.

**From inside.** A lightness. The world stops feeling like a box He is stuck in, or a place He is missing from. Some feel held without being hemmed in.

**At the edge.** It reaches it: "none grasps Him", not even His own wisdom and delight in His Essence (line 143, and line 147). Two opposites in one subject is where the mind stops.

**Into the day.** Put on your coat today and think: "He is in the world like this, and is never caught."

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §14: the soul's own powers, which it can decline to be moved by (lines 144-147)

**The movement.** It turns inward to the powers of the soul before they reach the body: will, delight, wisdom, the wish to win. It finds the soul both wholly caught up in them and free of them. The mind glimpses a self that is more than its moods.

> וכמו שאנו רואי' בחוש שאם לא תחפוץ הנפש להטות א"ע להתפעלות ענג או צער לא תתפעל כלל וכלל
> *As we see plainly: if the soul does not wish to lean itself toward the stir of pleasure or pain, it is not stirred at all.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the soul in itself is simple, "free of every quality, even of will and delight" (line 145). Yet it can want, delight, think, win and glory. How? The powers are planted in it as roots, hidden, and come out when something calls them (line 145). In a child the power of wisdom is a root; in an old man who has gained wisdom, it shines always into his mind (line 146). Step by step: sometimes the whole soul is swept up in one delight or one pain, until it nearly faints away. That shows the power is truly its own (line 145). And yet, "if the soul does not wish to lean toward pleasure or pain, it is not stirred at all" (line 147). So the powers are also its garments, which it can take off (line 147). Above: His ten qualities in His Essence are His, and still "no one grasps Him", not even His own wisdom and delight. They are garments He never has to remove (line 147). Bring it home. Think of a mood that has taken you over this week. Then notice that you are more than it: you can decline to lean into it. That is the soul's freedom the text describes, not by force, but by not wanting. The picture holds a long way. It breaks where the text says: our garments can be taken off; His are not even apart from Him.

**From inside.** A little freedom. A mood still comes, but it is not the whole of you. Behind it, a quieter self that can choose where to lean.

**At the edge.** It points there: the soul's essence, "free of every quality", pictures His Essence, "one, but not in number, even in hidden root" (line 147). The mind can notice the soul's freedom; it cannot see the soul's essence directly.

**Into the day.** When a strong mood rises today, say quietly: "I do not have to lean into this."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §15: "In Your light we see light"; a candle at noon (lines 148-152)

**The movement.** It gathers all the careful sorting of the last sections into one plain conclusion: there is no difference between the One-alone and the One-of-all. Then it gives a picture that makes the hiding itself visible: a candle that only shows when the sun goes down.

> ונמצ' מובן מכל זה דאין הפרש כלל בין בחי' יחיד לבחי' אחד
> *So it is understood from all this that there is no difference at all between Yachid and Echad.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: however the lights come from Him, spread out, clothed, or unveiled from hiding, "there is nothing outside the Essence" (line 149). Even the vessels are His; "He and His causes are one" (line 149). Step by step: "With You is the source of life" speaks of His hidden qualities, "nullified and included in His very Essence" (line 149). "In Your light we see light" speaks of those same qualities when they come out into vessels. And one explains the other: because the source is "with You", what is seen in vessels is still "Your light" (line 150). So "there is no difference at all between Yachid and Echad" (line 150). Even after the contraction, the light of the line is not separated from Him; "darkness and light are the same before Him" (line 150). The text's picture: a candle at noon (line 151). In the full sun its light is there but cannot be seen. When the sun withdraws, the candle shows. So "Your light" was already there in the hidden; the contraction is what lets it show as a light of its own (lines 151-152). Bring it home. Light a candle in daylight and look at it. It is burning, and you can hardly see it. Then think: the world's own light, which seems so separate, is like that candle inside His sun. The picture breaks: a candle is a second light next to the sun. Here the candle's light is His light too.

**From inside.** A resting. The long argument lands. Many feel a gentle relief that the world and He are not two after all.

**At the edge.** Yes. "With You" means included in His very Essence, before any vessel (line 149). The mind can say "no difference", but cannot see from the side where there is none.

**Into the day.** Light a candle this Friday before sunset and watch how little it shows, then how much after dark.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §16: "our God"; the inside of the cup (lines 153-155)

**The movement.** It asks why "our God" stands between the two Havayahs, and answers with a cup. The inside of a cup is one with what it holds; the outside is only for holding. Souls come from the inside. The mind finds that "our" is not a word of ownership but of oneness.

> הנה העיקר הוא בית חללו דווקא ששם המשקה מתאחד בו ואין הבדל בין המשקה לדופני חלל הכוס המחזיקו
> *The main thing is its hollow, for there the drink becomes one with it, and there is no difference between the drink and the walls of the cup that hold it.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: wisdom is called "alive" and understanding "life". A seed holds all the limbs hidden; in the mother they grow into a whole form (line 153). "Our God" is understanding, God come out into parts, "in every divine soul by its measure" (line 153). Step by step, the picture: a cup has an inside and an outside. The inside, its hollow, is the real vessel; there the wine and the walls are as one. The outside is only for the hand to lift it (line 153). A knife is all outside: it works only by passing on the hand's motion. A telescope lets the eye see farther than it can by itself (line 154). An eye, a hand, a mind each have an inside, ready to receive sight, motion, wisdom, even before they act, and an outside, what they do (line 154). Souls come from the inside of the vessels above; angels from the outside (line 153). That is why a person is called "adam", likeness: the seed comes from the very essence of the mind, before it thinks anything, and so it makes a whole person like the one who made it (lines 154-155). So "our God": Israel, which rose in His first thought, is what makes Yachid and Echad one. "All is one from the side of Israel, which rose in thought and is the intermediary" (line 155). Bring it home. Hold a cup of water. Feel the outside in your hand. Look at the inside, where the water and the cup meet with no gap. That inside, the text says, is where you come from. The picture breaks only because a cup can be emptied; the soul's inside is never empty of Him.

**From inside.** A settled belonging. Not "I own a God" but "I come from the inside of Him". Some feel less like a tool and more like a child.

**At the edge.** It points to it: the soul's root is in "the essence of His primordial wisdom in His very Essence" (line 155). The mind can hold the cup; the source of the hollow is beyond it.

**Into the day.** At a meal today, before you drink, look into the cup for a moment and say "our God".

**Ladder.** L01 (Under everything, the spark)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §17: "Baruch Shem", and "When is He great? In the city of our God" (lines 156-159)

**The movement.** It moves the weight of the Shema from its lofty first verse to the quiet line said under the breath. It sends the mind to know God through the created world, the very place where He seems most hidden, and says He is greatest there.

> עכ"ז אדרבה עיקר מצות הידיעה לידע האמת איך שהוי"ה ואלקים כולא חד ממש
> *Even so, on the contrary: the main commandment of knowing is to know the truth, how Havayah and Elokim are entirely one.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the tribes said to Jacob, "just as there is only One in your heart, so in ours", and Jacob answered "Baruch Shem" (line 157). In the Temple it was said aloud; now, in exile, quietly, with the Shechinah "lying in the dust". Still, "the main unity is in Baruch Shem" (line 157). Step by step: why do the morning Psalms praise God through creatures, sun, sea, animals, and not through the lofty worlds? Because "more of the praise of the Essence" is found in the lower unity (line 158). And the command to know is not "know Havayah" but "know that Havayah is Elokim": the high Name one with the Name of the world (line 158). From His works, the "name of action", "the Holy One Himself" is known, though no thought grasps Him (line 158). The Sages ask of "Great is God in the city of our God": "When is He great? When He is in the city of our God" (line 159). The city is the endless joinings of letters that build the worlds, a city without walls (line 159). That is why the text says the lower unity has an advantage, even in exile, "with more hiding of the Face", over the Temple (line 159). Bring it home. Sing one line of Pesukei deZimra today about a created thing, rain or grass or the sea, and know that you are praising Him more there, not less. The picture of the city breaks: a city is built by many hands. This one is built by one Speaker, without end.

**From inside.** Pleasure in ordinary things as signs of Him. A lifting of the old idea that the physical world is a distraction. Some feel exile itself become meaningful.

**At the edge.** It points there: "no thought grasps His Essence", and yet He Himself is known through His works (line 158). The mind knows the works; the Knower beyond them is not an object.

**Into the day.** Look at one created thing today, a tree, the sky, a child, and say: "Havayah is Elokim, here."

**Ladder.** L07 (Know it today, bring it to the heart)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §18: "These are His mighty acts": the hiding that makes a world (lines 160-163)

**The movement.** It explains why the lower unity outranks the higher: only there does something truly new come from nothing, through a real screen. The mind is asked to see God's strength not in shining but in hiding completely.

> והן הן גבורותיו שיוכל לצמצם ולהסתיר לגמרי כדי שיהי' בריאה יש
> *And these are His mighty acts: that He can contract and hide completely, so that there can be a created "something".*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: above, everything is His light unveiled from hiding; nothing is wholly new (line 160). Below the screen, the world is "new from its root", created from nothing. So "the power of the Essence is found there more" (line 161). "Like the advantage of light out of darkness", a real advantage (line 160). Step by step: two sayings of creation. "Let there be light" is kindness: His essential wish to do good, so strong it breaks through every screen and shines in the lowest world "as in the kingship of the Infinite" (line 162). "Kindness does not thicken" as it goes down (line 162). That is why "as they are one above, so below, and not more" (line 162). "Let there be a firmament" is strength: a real screen, a complete hiding. What comes through it is "a completely new light" (line 162). "These are His mighty acts: that He can contract and hide completely, so that there can be a created something" (line 162). And this strength, the text says, is higher than the first kindness. It is the reason the end outranks the beginning. Bring it home. Look at yourself: a separate "I", with your own thoughts and will. That you can feel separate at all is His greatest strength at work. It is not a failure of His presence. The picture of light and firmament holds; it breaks only because a firmament in the sky is a thing. His hiding is not a thing between you and Him; it is Him.

**From inside.** Awe of a new kind: not at brightness, but at the power to be so hidden. Some feel their own existence as something given with great care.

**At the edge.** Yes. The text calls it "strength within kindness", above the first wish to create (line 162). The mind can see the result of the hiding, a world; it cannot see the Hider.

**Into the day.** Say "You are hiding here" once today about something that seems completely without God.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §19: the King exalted alone; the will inside every detail of the deed (lines 164-166)

**The movement.** It shows the mind that a will and its deed are not two. Every detail of what was wanted appears in what is done. So the deed is not a poor copy of God's will but the will itself, carried out.

> ולפי"ז אין הפרש בין המהות של עצם רצונו קודם שיבא לידי מעשה לרצון שבגוף המעשה
> *So there is no difference between the essence of his will before it comes to deed and the will within the body of the deed.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the firmament that divides is made of both sides, like a parable that brings a deep idea down (line 164). Above it stands "the King who alone is exalted, lifted above the days of the world"; yet "God reigns, He is clothed in majesty" (line 164). "Forever, God, Your word stands in the heavens" (line 164). Step by step, the text's picture is a builder. First he wants a building: the whole, with all its reasons and details, in one will. Then he stops simply wanting and starts building. The work is separate from the wanting, and yet every detail comes out exactly as it was wanted, "not a thing more, not a thing less" (line 166). A will never exists alone. It is always a will for something: for an idea to lean one way, for a love, for a deed (line 165). So a will and its deed are one. Or picture a person drawing on a slate: his hand's motion follows every detail of the picture in his mind (line 166). Bring it home. When you do a mitzvah with your hands, you are not doing an echo of His will. The text says the will is in the deed, detail by detail. The picture breaks at one point: a human builder's hands can slip. His deed never departs from His will.

**From inside.** A seriousness about details, and a quiet joy in them. The sense that doing a thing exactly is a way of touching the one who wanted it.

**At the edge.** It points to "the will in itself", which is "set apart" from coming down even to be a source (line 164, and line 172). The mind meets the will in the deed; the will before the deed is hidden from it.

**Into the day.** Do one small task today exactly, every step, and say: "Your will is in this."

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §20: "All that God desired, He did"; "You are He, after creation as before" (lines 167-170)

**The movement.** It gathers the whole world into one finished work and says that God is the same before and after it. Then it shows why the finished deed holds the inner wish more than anything before it: the end is where everything is complete.

> והיינו פי' אתה הוא משנבר' כו' ואתה הוא קודם שנברא כו' בלי שינוי כלל כי אין המעשה נבדל מן הרצון
> *This is the meaning of "You are He after the world was created, and You are He before it was created", with no change at all, for the deed is not separate from the will.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "All that God desired He did, in heaven and on earth, in the seas and all the depths" (line 167). Everything He wanted for Himself came out, detail by detail, into creation (lines 167-168). "Blessed is He who spoke and the world came to be": the speaking is the hidden wish, and at once "the world was" (line 168). Step by step: so "You are He after creation as before, with no change", because the deed is not separate from the will, just as the hand's motion is not separate from the wish in the mind (line 168). Then the inner wish. "Desire" (chefetz) is the inside of the will (line 168). A will for kindness passes through reasons, but its heart is the kind deed itself; if the deed could be done without the reasons, the will would be just as complete (lines 168-169). And the end is best not because it is last, but because in the finished work everything is included, as a builder's joy comes only when the whole house stands (line 169). "God saw all that He had made, and behold it was very good" (line 170). Bring it home. Think of a job you finished. The pleasure came at the end, not because the last brick was special, but because the whole was there. The text says your finished deeds hold God's inner wish in the same way. The picture breaks: a builder goes home. He renews the work each day, and His fulfilled wish is the text's reason for it (line 170).

**From inside.** Satisfaction in completing things, felt as sacred. A calm about God's changelessness: nothing you see has altered Him.

**At the edge.** It points to "the will for itself", above any coming-out (line 168). The mind knows the finished work; the One who is "He before" and "He after" it knows Himself alone.

**Into the day.** Finish one thing today that you have left half-done, and say "very good".

## Shaar Kriat Shema §21: heaven, earth and sea; gathering the waters to speak (lines 171-174)

**The movement.** It reads the third day of creation as the way a mind becomes speech. The thoughts must be gathered and narrowed, "to one place", before dry land, a spoken word, can appear. It also names the souls that live inside the hidden sea, wholly given over.

> והנה כאשר האדם רוצה להביא מחשבתו ורצונו שבלבו לגלוי הדבור והמעש' מוכרח שיפסיק ויצמצ' א"ע מטרדת מחשבתו
> *When a person wants to bring his thought and the will in his heart out into speech and deed, he must stop and contract himself from the busyness of his thinking.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: heaven is the firmament that hides the will-for-itself so that a world can come to be (lines 171-172). Earth is the end of the deed, the inner desire, and so "earth was created first" in one opinion (line 172). The sea is the hidden world, and the dry land the revealed one; "whatever is on land is in the sea", only covered (line 173). Step by step: the souls that live in the letters of thought are "like fish swallowed in the great sea", nullified in their very being (line 173). That is where a person gives his soul "in echad", as R. Akiva did (line 173). Then the picture. When you want to say what is in your heart, you must stop thinking about everything at once and gather yourself. If you do not want to speak, the thought stays whole and hidden. But once you have gathered it, "you hide nothing": all of it comes out, in the shape of words (line 173). So "let the waters gather to one place, and let the dry land appear" (line 173). A teacher who wants to reach a small child must shrink himself to what the child can take (line 174). Bring it home. Before you speak to someone today, especially about something that matters, stop and gather. Let the many thoughts become one. Then speak. The picture breaks: our gathering loses some of what was in us. The text says His loses nothing; every letter of the hidden thought comes out.

**From inside.** A collected calm before speaking. The words come fewer and truer. Some feel what it might be to be a fish in a sea, not apart from where you live.

**At the edge.** It touches it: the souls "swallowed in the sea" are in "essential bitul", one with His qualities (line 173). The mind can gather itself to speak; that deeper swallowing is beyond its doing.

**Into the day.** Before one real conversation today, be silent for a few seconds, gather yourself, then speak one clear sentence.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §22: heaven and earth created as one; the seed that rots and rises (lines 175-178)

**The movement.** It turns the mind from the sky to the soil. The heavens keep their fixed law and add nothing. The earth, lowest of all, is where something truly new keeps coming into being. The mind learns to look down for the wonder of creation.

> אבל הנה בארץ אנו רואי' דבר חדש שיש בה כח התחדשות הבריאה בהתהוות מציאות חדשה ממש
> *But in the earth we see something new: it has the power of renewing creation, bringing into being a truly new existence.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Heaven and earth were created as one": that speaks of a source above both orders, where neither comes first (line 175). The heavens praise Him and keep the law He set, "a statute He gave, which shall not pass" (line 176). The stars do not grow in number. Their daily renewal "is not new at root", only fresh light, like the daily renewal of a person's life (line 176). Step by step, the earth. Sow a grain of wheat: it rots and becomes nothing, and then many new grains come up (line 176). Grass and trees grow even unsown. Springs rise without end. Rain itself comes from vapors that rose from the earth (line 176). The text asks where this power lives, and answers: in the dust itself, "all came from the dust" (line 177). It admits a puzzle. The Sages say every blade of grass has a star that strikes it and says "grow", and rain and sun matter too (line 177). Yet on the third day, before sun or rain or farmer, "the earth brought forth grass" by its own power (line 178). "Both are true" (line 178). Bring it home. Hold a seed, or a piece of bread made from seed. That grain was once nothing, rotted in the ground, and came back many. The text says the power of real beginning is here, underfoot. The picture of the seed breaks where the text leaves it: a seed needs rain and sun. The earth's power, it says, is its own, and more than that, it is from the first Beginning, which the next section explains.

**From inside.** A reverence for soil, bread, the ordinary ground. Some feel their own "rotting" times, when they felt like nothing, become part of a new growing.

**At the edge.** It points toward it without reaching it here: where the earth's endless newness comes from is the next section's secret.

**Into the day.** Before eating bread today, say "who brings forth bread from the earth" and think of the grain that became nothing first.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §23: the end holds the power of the beginning (lines 179-182)

**The movement.** It gives the reason for all the talk of the lower being higher. What is lowest, with nothing beneath it, carries the power of the very first beginning. The mind is shown water rebounding from the bottom of a mountain, and a mother growing a child from almost nothing.

> הוא מבחי' כחו של אא"ס שבתחלה כי הארץ סוף הכל שאין עוד למטה הימנה לכך נמצא בה כח התחלה
> *It is from the power of the Infinite at the beginning: because the earth is the end of all, with nothing below it, therefore the power of the beginning is found in it.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "A generation goes, a generation comes, and the earth stands forever." The earth can make new things from nothing, which the higher elements cannot, "because their beginning is wedged in their end" (line 179). Step by step, the pictures. Water runs down from a spring high on a mountain; at the bottom, with nowhere further to go, it rises again with force, back up to its source. Throw a stone hard against a wall; the same force comes back to you. And the force that returns is stronger than the force that went out (line 179). Then a woman bearing a child. The father's seed is received and becomes as nothing, like wheat rotting in the ground, and from the mother's own power a whole child grows, limb by limb (line 179). Yet not without "a divine power": "for I am holy", the Shechinah between them, the third partner (lines 179-180). So "in the end of all is found the power of the beginning, and more: it makes new from nothing without stopping" (line 180). The same with Torah: the Oral Torah brings new laws with no hint in the Written, as a child grows in its mother (line 181). And the unions above are pictured as man and woman, not as a rich man giving to a poor one, because they come "from the power of the Infinite Himself" (line 181). Bring it home. Where are you lowest? The place in your life with nothing below it. The text says that is where the power of beginning waits. The picture breaks: water and stones only return; here the end gives birth to what never was.

**From inside.** A reversal felt in the body: the bottom is not a dead end but a springboard. Hope that is not wishful, because it rests on how creation works.

**At the edge.** Yes. The earth's power is "from the power of the Infinite at the beginning" (line 179), before any light or chain. The mind cannot see that beginning; it can only find it at the bottom.

**Into the day.** Name your lowest point today and say: "Here is the power of the beginning."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §24: the soul sown in the body; weeping that reaches deeper (lines 183-186)

**The movement.** It takes the soul's sorrow in the body and turns it into a sowing. The weeping of a soul far from home, it says, reaches deeper into God's delight than the soul's delight did when it was near. The mind is asked not to waste its own tears.

> ובכי' זו מגעת לעומק הענג האלקי יותר ממה שהי' עונג אלקי בתחלה
> *And this weeping reaches the depth of the divine delight more than the divine delight that was there at the beginning.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Baruch Shem" has an extra advantage over the first verse, and the text brings three proofs: the earth's endless newness, the Oral Torah that grows new laws, and souls sown in bodies (line 183). The love "with all your might", without limit, belongs only to a soul in a body; it did not have it above (line 183). Step by step, the picture is sowing. "Who sows a measure except to reap many?" (line 183). A grain grows only after it rots completely and becomes as nothing (line 185). So the soul, planted in a body, lives a life of pain; the body darkens it, until it seems swallowed, "rotted" (line 185). And just then it grows: it becomes bitter and weeps at its distance. "And this weeping reaches the depth of the divine delight more than the delight that was there at the beginning" (line 185). The text gives a proof from life: the deepest love of a father for his son shows itself not in good times but in his son's trouble, "my heart yearns for him" (line 185). R. Shimon bent and kissed the dust, because through the body's crushing the soul rises higher (line 185). So "Baruch Shem" in exile is higher than in the Temple: the souls "come with weeping", and in this weeping is "the root of the depth of delight" (line 186). Bring it home. Remember a time you wept over being far from God, or far from who you should be. The text says that tear went deeper than your best moment. The picture of the seed breaks: a seed does not know it is rotting. You do, and your knowing is part of what makes it grow.

**From inside.** Dignity for your sadness. Not wallowing: a sense that the ache itself is a way in. Often, afterward, a joy with roots.

**At the edge.** Yes. The weeping reaches "above its first root", "before the King", beyond where the soul began (line 186). The mind cannot measure this depth; it is felt as longing.

**Into the day.** If you feel far today, say it to Him in one honest sentence, and let it be a seed.

**Ladder.** L12 (Darkness turned to light)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §25: the yoke of heaven; the king whose word is done (lines 187-190)

**The movement.** It lowers the mind from understanding to simple acceptance, and finds that the lower reaches higher. The stars bow by turning, and a king is pleased most not by eager subjects but by subjects whose own will is gone. Plain obedience touches the Essence.

> שמעוררי' נח"ר לפני עצמותו שא' ונעשה רצונו
> *They arouse delight before His Essence: that He spoke and His will was done.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Baruch Shem" is the time for accepting the yoke of heaven, "you shall surely set a king over you" (line 187). How can that be higher, if it is only acceptance? Because the higher fear, true self-nullification, "hangs on" the lower fear, bare acceptance (line 187). Step by step, the stars. The spheres turn without stopping, and that turning is their bowing toward the Shechinah, "the host of heaven bows to You" (line 188). The soul of a sphere is humbled only by what it grasps of the divine power that makes it. But its body, bowing with its whole being, reaches "the Essence of Godliness above the contraction that makes it from nothing" (line 188). The text's picture: a coronation. The officers and the people bow and say "be our ruler". The king is pleased and agrees to rule. But what truly delights him is afterward, when his every word is done, not because they want it but because he said it (line 189). From that giving-up of will comes "the crown within the crown" (line 189). So the mitzvot done under the yoke "arouse delight before His Essence, that He spoke and His will was done", and from this the World to Come is made (line 189). Bring it home. Think of a mitzvah you do not understand or do not feel like doing. Do it today simply because He said so. The picture of the king breaks: an earthly king can be flattered. He is not; He delights only in the truth of the bowing.

**From inside.** A strange lightness in obeying without needing to feel it. The tired self can rest in simply doing. Some notice a deep quiet under the plain act.

**At the edge.** Yes. The yoke reaches "the crown within the crown", "reckoned as the Essence of the Emanator" (line 190). Understanding cannot get there; acceptance does.

**Into the day.** Do one mitzvah today that you do not feel, and say: "Because You said so."

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §26: "You were shown, to know": begin with heaven and earth (lines 191-194)

**The movement.** It gives a plain instruction for the whole path: begin with the simplest contemplation, how heaven and earth come from nothing. It promises that this humble start is as good as contemplating the Essence, and warns that without it nothing higher will take root.

> ה"ז כמו שמתבונן ממש בעצמות אא"ס שלפני האצילות בלי שינוי כלל
> *It is as if he were contemplating the very Essence of the Infinite before emanation, with no change at all.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "You were shown, to know" works through God's kingship making a separate world, and "from this in particular one comes to know His very Essence" (line 191). The power that makes the lowest world is the same will that rose in Him first, "one thought", with no change between the highest world and the lowest (line 191). Step by step, the rule. "A person should train himself to deepen his mind first only in simple contemplation of the creation of heaven and earth and all their host, from nothing to something" (line 191). That is what the Psalms and the morning praises do. And "one who contemplates the divine power that forms the heavens and the earth is as if he were contemplating the very Essence before emanation, with no change at all" (line 191). The warning: whoever does not fix this in himself firmly, all his toil in the details of the higher worlds "will not fix anything in his soul" (line 192). The order for beginners: the elements from the stars, the stars from the angels above them, up to the kingship that makes the world (line 192). His providence, "the eyes of God roam the whole earth", watches each creature (line 192). The text's picture: a person who wants to build. First the wish, then the know-how belonging to this building, then the leaning toward generous or careful, then the words and orders, then the deed (line 192). A tzaddik who stands above nature can change even the letters of this lowest world, "He who told oil to burn will tell vinegar to burn" (line 194). Bring it home. Tonight, look at the sky, or even the ceiling and the floor. Think only: these were not, and now they are, from Him. Stay with that before anything grander.

**From inside.** Relief at a starting place you can actually stand on. A plain wonder at existence. Over days, a firm floor under all later thoughts.

**At the edge.** Yes, and startlingly: this simple thought is "as if" contemplating the Essence before emanation (line 191). The mind stays with heaven and earth; what it touches through them is beyond it.

**Into the day.** Each night this week, look up once and say: "From nothing, by You, now."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §27-28: the thrown stone; the word that never leaves the Speaker (lines 195-201)

**The movement.** It walks the mind down to the very last rung of the worlds, where things move like a thrown stone, far from the hand. Then it shows why the picture fails: God's word is never outside Him, so even the last rung is held and made new.

> משום שאין זה דבר שחוץ ממנו כדבור האדם
> *Because it is not a thing outside Him, like a person's speech.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Baruch Shem kevod malchuto le'olam va'ed" is laid over the worlds word by word, down to "le'olam va'ed", the lowest world; and "va'ed" is "echad" with its letters changed, "all His essential desire is in her" (line 195). Every world has thought, speech and deed inside it, down to "deed of deed", the farthest edge (lines 195-197). (The file marks a new section, 28, at line 199.) Step by step, the picture. A hand moving is one with its strength. But throw a stone: it flies far on the hand's force, separated from the hand, until the force runs out (line 197). The turning of the spheres is like that: not their own choice, but carried by a divine force around them (line 199). Then the text breaks its own picture. The stone's body was not made by the throw. The spheres' very bodies are made from nothing by that force (line 199). And a person's word, once said, is gone from him. God renews creation every day "because it is not a thing outside Him, like a person's speech" (line 200). Miracles prove it: the letters can be changed at will (line 200). At the very bottom, the forces of evil receive nothing even from this last divine power. They draw only from its waste, "hair and nails", while the spheres bow "without separation, ever" (line 201). Bring it home. Throw something light, a crumpled paper, into a basket. Watch it leave your hand and go on its own. Then think: nothing in the world has left His hand like that.

**From inside.** A deep security. Even the farthest, most mechanical things are held. The sense of being "thrown" into the world softens into being carried.

**At the edge.** It points there: His word "is not outside Him", so the lowest deed is still in the Speaker. The mind can see the stone; it cannot see a speech that never leaves the mouth.

**Into the day.** When you speak a promise today, keep it, as a small copy of a word that does not leave its speaker.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §29: the absent-minded doer, the practiced hand, and the dream (lines 202-204)

**The movement.** It studies what happens when we act without thinking: the distracted fidget, the expert's habit, the dream. It finds that a well-worn habit is not empty. All the thought once poured into it is still hidden inside.

> ונמצ' גם בחי' הפנימי' יש בהעלם מצד שהוקבע ברגילות
> *So the inner part too is there, hidden, because it has been fixed by habit.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: a thrown stone carries no thought at all after it leaves the hand (line 202). But a person can also work with his hands while his mind is far away: doodle perfect letters while deep in talk, play an instrument while chatting, weave or write "without any intention" (line 203). Step by step: there are two very different kinds. One is the distracted doer, so absorbed elsewhere that he later cannot believe he did it; only the faintest trace of mind is in his hands (line 203). The other is the master who works by long habit. He does not need to think now, because for years he poured all his inner thought into this craft. "So the inner part too is there, hidden, fixed by habit" (line 204). Like a student fluent in a halacha: when he recites it with little thought, all his past study is inside the words (line 204). Dreams are the text's picture for exile: the outermost scrap of the day's thinking, rearranged strangely. "We were like dreamers." And Joseph, the interpreter of dreams, sets the letters right (line 203). Bring it home. Think of a prayer or blessing you say by habit. Do not despise it. If you once said it with your whole heart, that heart is hidden in it now. And if you have not yet, today's attention is what will be stored in tomorrow's habit. The picture breaks for the distracted doer: nothing is stored there. The text keeps the two apart.

**From inside.** A kinder view of routine. Less guilt about rote prayer, more desire to fill it once more. Some notice that their practiced hands carry more love than they thought.

**At the edge.** It does not reach the edge here. It is a study of the lowest rung, preparing the next section's picture of God's craft.

**Into the day.** Choose one blessing you say by habit and say it once today with full attention, to store it.

**Ladder.** L14 (The unity in your hand)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §30: the master craftsman; the whole mind in the finger's hint (lines 205-208)

**The movement.** It turns to the best kind of making, where all of a craftsman's wisdom is in each detail of the work. It asks the mind to see creation this way, and to read in any small thing the whole mind of its Maker.

> אך ודאי שמלובש כל עצם השכל האלקי העליון ששייך לכל אשר עשה גם בכל פרט ופרט
> *But surely the whole essence of the divine mind that belongs to all He made is clothed in each and every detail.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the innermost level of making is the craftsman's wisdom, "wisdom of heart", as Bezalel had, who "knew how to join the letters by which heaven and earth were made" (lines 205-206). All his inner thought is in each detail of the form (line 205). Step by step: a hint made with a finger can carry a whole deep idea, "for the wise, a hint is enough", more than a written page can (line 206). Writing carries the idea only when all its words come together; a gesture carries it whole (line 206). So "how many are Your works, God; You made them all in wisdom" (line 206). "How great are Your works, how very deep Your thoughts" (line 207). The whole divine mind that belongs to creation is clothed "in each and every detail" (line 207). And like a master who works from long practice, He renews creation daily; and "He looked into the Torah and created the world", so one who repeats a halacha holds up the world, and "since the Temple fell, the Holy One has in His world only the four cubits of halacha" (lines 207-208). Bring it home. Pick up one small thing: a leaf, a stone, a spoon. Look at it the way you would look at a master's work, knowing that the whole mind that made it is in this one detail. The picture breaks: a craftsman's work is separate from him once finished. His work is renewed by Him each moment, and never leaves Him.

**From inside.** Attention sharpens. Small things become interesting, even beautiful. A quiet sense of being surrounded by intention.

**At the edge.** It points there: this is "wisdom in Asiyah", the lowest wisdom; its root is "the highest wisdom" (line 207). The mind sees the detail; the Mind behind it is more than it can hold.

**Into the day.** Once today, hold one small object for a minute and say: "Your whole wisdom is in this."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §31: speech, its body and its soul; no letter is lost (lines 209-212)

**The movement.** It holds up a mirror to our words. Some carry heart and mind; some are empty, like stones. Yet not one letter is lost. The mind is turned to the power and responsibility of speech, and through it to the speech that makes the world.

> שהרי לא יאבד אפי' אות א' הגם שיצא באויר
> *For not even one letter is lost, even though it went out into the air.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: speech has a body and a soul. The body is the breath and voice, shaped by throat, tongue, teeth and lips (lines 209-210). The soul is the thought and feeling inside the words (line 210). A newborn has lips and a tongue but cannot speak; the power of speech is in the soul (line 209). Step by step, three kinds of words. Words from the heart, full of life, which move the heart of the listener. Words said with little thought, like answering a small question while busy with a large one. And empty words, "idle chatter", "like stones", with no life at all (line 211). Words spoken in sleep carry not even a trace of mind (line 211). And still, "not even one letter is lost, though it went out into the air". Words of Torah fly upward; evil speech and lies need repair (line 211). Above, "By the word of God the heavens were made." His word has its inner thought, its feeling, and its going-out: "My word that goes out of My mouth" (line 211). And through the Torah we speak, He "sits and learns", then judges, then feeds the world (line 211). Bring it home. Listen to yourself for one hour today. Which words were stones? Which were full? The text is not asking for silence; it is asking that your words carry something.

**From inside.** A new respect for the mouth. Fewer careless words, and a small pleasure in a word said well. Some feel a sobering at the thought that nothing said is lost.

**At the edge.** It does not reach the edge here. It is a map of speech that prepares the next sections on thought and the three ways light passes.

**Into the day.** Say one full sentence of Torah, or of kindness, aloud today, knowing it is not lost.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §32: the floors of thought; stray thoughts like chaff (lines 213-215)

**The movement.** It maps the mind's own rooms, from deep study to idle daydream to the stray thoughts that drift in unwanted. It treats the stray ones gently and honestly, as chaff that blows away, and saves its seriousness for the words we speak.

> ולכך הרהור לא עביד מידי אבל דברים בטלים צריכי' ביעור בנהר דינור
> *So a passing thought does nothing, but idle words need to be burned away in the river of fire.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: thought has floors too. The lowest is the thinking that prepares words: "how shall I say this?" Above it is thought for itself: hearing another's words in your mind, picturing shapes, and above that, the thinking that rises from the heart in love or anger, and above that, deep study (lines 213-215). Step by step, the stray thoughts. Some thoughts fall into the mind about honor, money, other people's desires, that a person does not even want. They have a little life, only because the soul once cared for such things; by habit they come back (line 214). The text calls them "chaff and straw": straw that once held grain, now empty, blown off by the wind (line 214). And its comfort: "a passing thought does nothing", while idle words, once spoken, fly out and need to be cleansed (line 214). Thoughts with some life in them can become accusers; those with none "will be lost and scattered" (line 215). Then the whole chain above: deep thought, feeling, and the lowest letters of thought become the source of speech, and speech the source of deed. Each day He "sits and learns" (thought), "sits and judges" (speech), "sits and feeds" (deed), and on Shabbat and new moons everything rises again (line 215). Bring it home. When a stray thought drifts in during prayer, do not fight it or follow it. The text says it is chaff. Let it blow away, and come back to the words. The picture breaks where it should: chaff blows away by itself; your part is only not to hold on.

**From inside.** Less anxiety about the mind's noise. A lighter hand with oneself. More care about what comes out of the mouth.

**At the edge.** It does not reach the edge here. This is a map of the mind, used to build the chain of worlds.

**Into the day.** When a stray thought comes in prayer today, say inwardly "chaff" and return to the next word.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §33: screen, hole and window; a whole essence in the smallest part (lines 216-219)

**The movement.** It gives the mind three ways light can pass: through a screen, a hole, a window. Then it names the hole as the way the soul receives: a narrow point, but of the Giver's own essence, which can grow until it equals its source.

> שכל עצם בלתי מתחלק דהיינו שגם בחלק הקטן האחרון שבו ימצא בו הכל
> *For every essence is indivisible: even in its smallest, last part, all of it is found.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: light passes in three ways (line 216). Through a screen: what comes through is utterly changed, "a glow of a glow", cut off from its source. Through a hole: a narrow point of the giver's own essence gets through. Through a window: it passes "as from vessel to vessel", whole (lines 216-220). Step by step, the hole. A great teacher cannot pour all his mind into a small child; he gives one point, the most the child can hold. But that point is his own mind (line 217). "For every essence is indivisible: even in its smallest, last part, all of it is found" (line 217). So a spark fanned by bellows becomes a great flame; a sapling becomes a cedar; a one-year-old becomes a sage of seventy; a student can come to stand where his teacher stood, even "a double portion of your spirit" (line 217). The soul, "God's lamp", is such a spark, "an essential part" (line 217). It can grow in this body until it matches its root above (line 217). Angels are different: like a pool cut off from its spring, they stay as they are (line 218). Bring it home. What you know of God may feel like a pinhole. The text says the pinhole is not a copy; it is the real light, only narrow. It can widen. The picture breaks at the screen: some light truly is cut off. The text does not pretend otherwise. But the soul, it says, is not behind a screen; it is at a hole.

**From inside.** Hope that feels solid. A sense of growth that is not self-improvement but widening. Some feel a quiet dignity: "the real thing is in me, small."

**At the edge.** It points there: the spark is "of the Essence", and "can grow to be like its source" (line 217). The mind can see the pinhole; it cannot measure how wide it will become.

**Into the day.** Take one small point of Torah today and turn it over a few times, fanning it like a spark.

**Ladder.** L01 (Under everything, the spark)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §34: the window; from a small hole, a great space (lines 220-222)

**The movement.** It shows the third way, the window, where a giver's whole mind passes to an equal. It promises that the soul's pinhole will one day be widened to hold all the light of the Essence. Then it lets the mind read the Maker in any made thing.

> הרי מנקב קטן יוכלו לעשות מרחב גדול עד שיוכלו להכיל כל אור העצמות
> *From a small hole a great space can be made, until it can hold all the light of the Essence.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: a window is when a wise man speaks to someone as wise as he: no shrinking, no short words, everything passes "as from vessel to vessel" (line 220). Moses to the seventy elders, Elijah to Elisha. In the future, "the light of the moon will be like the light of the sun", the receiver like the giver, "to be one in the One" (line 220). Step by step: now Israel receives only through a small hole, "peering through the lattice". But "from a small hole a great space can be made, until it can hold all the light of the Essence" (line 220). The picture: a master builder. When his deep plan comes down into speech and then into the actual building, nothing is lost. Once he is building, his whole wisdom is there in the work, "without any contraction" (line 221). A tzaddik can recognize a maker's very self from a tool he made, if the maker put his whole mind into it (line 222). So "you were shown, to know" through creation: "there is no difference here between the highest world before Atzilut and this low world" (line 222). The text adds a limit: this seeing belongs to Jewish souls, from their root (line 222). Bring it home. Look at your window at home. Light comes through it whole. Then think of the pinhole of your own knowing, and let yourself want it to become a window. The picture breaks: a window lets through light that was already outside. Here the widening is the light making room for itself in you.

**From inside.** Longing with expectation, not despair. Things around you seem signed by their Maker. Some feel a quiet impatience for the future, in the good sense.

**At the edge.** Yes. The future vessel will hold "all the light of the Essence", and the Shechinah will be "like the Infinite before the contraction" (line 220). The mind reaches that only as hope.

**Into the day.** Look at something beautiful that someone made and let it remind you of the Maker of all things.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §35: distance that lets things be; grasping that hides (lines 223-226)

**The movement.** It explains why there is distance between God and the worlds, and between the levels of a soul: without it, nothing else could exist. Then it turns the lamp on the mind itself. Understanding is a "something", and a something hides the true Nothing.

> כי השגה זו עצמה שהוא בחי' יש מסתרת אור עצמותו שהוא בחי' אין האמתי
> *For this grasping itself, being a "something", hides the light of His Essence, which is the true "nothing".*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: in a person, the will is far above the reason, the reason far above the feelings, the feelings far above speech. Each is "far" not in space but in rank (line 224). And the distance is needed: if the will shone right into the reason, the reason would vanish, "for there is no reason to the will" (line 224). Above, if the line of light came down "close", "all would be infinite, and there would be no ten sefirot at all" (line 224). Step by step: where there is "nothing", where the self is quiet, the Infinite is near. That is wisdom, "chochmah", "koach mah", the power of "what am I?". Moses said "what are we?", and so God spoke to him "face to face" (line 225). Understanding, binah, is a "something": I grasp, I know. And "this grasping itself, being a something, hides the light of His Essence, the true nothing" (line 225). The text's picture: seeing something far away through a mirror or a lens. It brings it closer, and yet "this closeness is a distance", because you see only what the glass shows, not the thing itself (line 225). And a window in the wall of a house: the walls hide and protect; the window lets in a measured light that fills the room (line 225). Bring it home. After you have understood something about God, notice the small satisfaction of "I get it". The text says that very "I" is a veil. Put the understanding down for a moment and stand there with nothing. The mirror picture breaks: a lens is outside you. The veil here is your own grasp.

**From inside.** A humbling that is also a relief. Less need to master God. A widening quiet where "what am I?" is not a despair but an opening.

**At the edge.** Yes. The "true nothing" of His Essence is hidden by any grasp at all (line 225). The mind can only reach it by becoming "mah", "what?".

**Into the day.** After learning today, say "and what am I?" and sit a moment without adding a thought.

## Shaar Kriat Shema §36: love that speaks; the screen made of both sides; the power of limit (lines 227-230)

**The movement.** It shows how little of the heart gets through into words, and how a screen, a vessel, a limit, all come from the same source as the light they hold. The mind is asked to find in every limit the unlimited One who can also limit.

> ורשימו זו היא בחי' כח הגבול שבאא"ס הבלתי מוגבל כי כל יכול הוא וגם כח הגבול ימצא מאתו
> *And this trace is the power of limit that is in the unlimited Infinite, for He is all-capable, and the power of limit too is found from Him.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: feelings reach speech only "through a small hole" (line 227). A loving friend talks a great deal with the one he loves; with someone he hates he cannot talk at all; "whoever did not speak to his friend three days is reckoned an enemy" (line 227). Yet words themselves are like stones; only a little of the heart's life shines through them (line 227). Step by step, the picture is love and its deeds. Love in the heart is the light; the kind deeds are its vessel. And the deeds go only as far as the love: a love with limits gives with limits, a father's boundless love gives without limit (lines 228-229). So light and vessel, though opposite, the spreading and the bounding, "come from one source", and run out together (line 229). The screen between the worlds is the same: made of the thickening of the light itself, both revealing and hiding, "from one source come two opposites" (line 228). And at the root, the trace left after the contraction is "the power of limit within the unlimited Infinite, for He is all-capable, and the power of limit too is from Him" (line 229). So a miracle happens inside nature; so the Ark stood in a room and took no room (line 229). Bring it home. Look at one limit in your life: your time, your strength, your means. The text does not call it the absence of God. It calls it His power too, the power to limit. The love-and-deed picture breaks at the top: our love runs out; the power that limits the world does not.

**From inside.** A peace with limits. Not resignation: a sense that the boundaries are held from inside by the same One who is beyond them.

**At the edge.** It reaches it: "the power of limit in the unlimited Infinite" (line 229), two opposites in one. The mind cannot fit them together; it can only honor both.

**Into the day.** At the end of your strength today, say: "You are here too, in my limit."

**Ladder.** L08 (What can't meet in me meets in Him)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §37: time is a creature; the Essence will shine in time (lines 231-234)

**The movement.** It lifts the mind out of time for a moment. Time itself was made, so "why did He not create earlier?" is an empty question. Then it brings the mind back into time with a promise: the One above time will shine in it, and time will not end.

> דהזמן עצמו הוא מחודש ונברא מאין ליש בבחי' גבול
> *For time itself is made new and created from nothing, in a bounded form.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: in the future, "Baruch Shem", God's kingship in time, will be above the Shema's first verse, because "the Essence of the Infinite, wholly above time, will shine in time", and time itself will become unending (line 231). "He will reign forever and ever." Step by step: the old kabbalists asked why creation began just then and not earlier. The true answer: the question is empty. "Time itself is new, created from nothing", like everything else; before it there was no before (line 232). A world, a year, a soul are made together (line 231). Then the Sages' saying: "Is there old age before the Holy One?" A person grows old because his days are counted and add up to a limit; the parts limit the whole. His days are not counted, so nothing adds up to old age (line 232). Above even that, His Essence is beyond time altogether, not even "the ancient of days" (line 233). The middle level, "Baruch Shem", is the Essence inside bounded time, which joins them (lines 233-234). Bring it home. Look at a clock. Every second it shows was created, as surely as the table under it. You are living inside a creature called time, held by One who is not inside it. The picture of the clock breaks: a clock measures a time that goes on without it. Here time itself is made, and could be made otherwise.

**From inside.** A loosening of time's grip. Hurry and dread soften a little. Some feel "now" as something given, not something running out.

**At the edge.** Yes. The Essence "wholly above time", before whom "who comes first?" has no meaning (line 233). The mind can only think in time; it can know that it is inside it.

**Into the day.** When you check the time today, add: "You made this minute."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §38: world, year, soul; the heartbeat that makes time (lines 235-237)

**The movement.** It finds the source of time inside the rhythm of life: the soul's going out and coming back, like the heart's two beats. The mind is asked to feel its own life as a pulse, and to see in the pulse the beginning of all time.

> והחיות רו"ש פי' רצוא מסתלק וחוזר מיד ומתפשט וחוזר ומסתלק וחוזר מיד ומתפשט
> *"And the living creatures ran and returned": running, withdrawing, and at once spreading out again, and again withdrawing, and at once spreading again.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: everything made has three sides, "world, year, soul": its body, the span of its lasting, and its life (line 235). A person, a tree, a sphere, the whole universe. Time is created together with the thing, not before it. Step by step, where does time come from? From the way the soul lives in anything: not only going out, not only drawing in, but both, "running and returning" (line 235). The picture is the heart: one beat pushes the breath of life out, the next draws it in, in a single moment; with only one, a person could not live (line 235). Or a candle flame, always rising and falling on its wick (line 235). What has withdrawn is past; what is spreading is future; and so "time" is born (line 235). From the Giver's side the order is reversed: "touching and not touching", reaching first and then holding back, like an eagle hovering over its nest (line 236). And the beginning and end of a thought are one: a man first wants to build, and then works out how; it is one thought, its start fixed in its end (lines 236-237). Bring it home. Your heart has beaten all day without your asking. Think of that once, as a fact, not something to watch: this going out and coming back is the text's own picture for the root of time. The picture breaks: your heartbeat keeps you in time. His "touching and not touching" is what makes time at all.

**From inside.** A tenderness toward one's own life, beat by beat. Time feels less like a line running out and more like a rhythm being given.

**At the edge.** It points to the root of time "in the Emanator Himself", before all worlds (line 236). The mind lives in the beats; it cannot stand where they begin.

**Into the day.** Once today, between one task and the next, say: "Running and returning, from You."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §39: the long face and the short face; the map of a great land (lines 238-241)

**The movement.** It shows the mind how a great, unhurried will becomes a small, exact deed, like a whole country drawn on one sheet of paper. And it untangles two orders that seem to clash: darkness first, or light first. Both are true, depending on who is moving.

> כמו כדור הארץ שמצוייר בלוח ונייר קטן שנהר ומדינה גדולה כמה מאות פרסאות נמשך בקו קצר במפה
> *Like the globe of the earth drawn on a small tablet or paper, where a river and a great country of many hundreds of miles is drawn as a short line on the map.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: the beginning of God's thought is "long", unhurried, without measure. Its end, where it comes out into the actual feelings and deeds of the world, is "short": exact, bounded (lines 238-239). So the level of the beginning is called "the long face" and the level of the end "the short face" (line 239). Step by step, the picture: a map. A river hundreds of miles long becomes a short line on paper; a builder reads that line and makes it long again (line 239). The deed is like the map: small, exact, and "a likeness of what was in the thought" (line 239). "Behold, place is with Me": all the directions of the world are gathered back into their beginning (line 239). Then two orders. When a person reaches up to God, he first runs out of himself and then returns: withdrawal, then spreading. When God gives to a person, He first reaches, then holds back so it can be received (lines 240-241). "In the beginning darkness, then light", yes, when the Giver is still hidden in Himself; but when He comes out to give, the light comes first (line 241). Bring it home. Look at a map of your city. Your whole daily life is a few lines there. The text says your small, exact deeds are like that: short lines that carry a vast intention. The map picture breaks: a map is a copy. Here the short line is the long intention itself, completed.

**From inside.** Peace about small lives. The large meaning is in the small lines. And a clearing of confusion about why God sometimes seems to come first and sometimes to wait.

**At the edge.** It points to "the beginning of the thought in the Essence", "in the manner of the Infinite" (line 239). The mind reads the map; it cannot hold the land.

**Into the day.** Do one small, exact deed today, and remember it is a line on a very large map.

**Ladder.** L04 (Held from inside and all around)

## Shaar Kriat Shema §40: "He consulted with the souls of the righteous" (lines 242-245)

**The movement.** It goes behind the first thought of creation to what moved it: a picture of the finished dwelling below, where souls in bodies do mitzvot. The mind learns that the end was the reason for the beginning, and that there a limit will become limitless.

> שזהו סוף מעש' שנתאוו' הקב"ה להיו' לו דירה בתחתוני' דוק' שעלה במ"ת פי' תחלה לתחלת המח'
> *This is the end of the deed: the Holy One desired a dwelling among the lowest, and this rose in thought first, meaning before the beginning of the thought.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: time's root is in God's "touching and not touching", and it comes out through the heart's running and returning into the worlds (lines 242-243). Above both, there is a third thing: what the thought is for (line 244). Step by step, the picture: a man wants to build a house. Why now? Because he pictured himself living in it, delighting in it, showing it to friends he loves. That picture came before the wish to build, and it is completed only when he actually lives there (line 244). So the Sages say "the end of the deed rose first in thought", not "in the beginning of the thought" but before it (lines 244-245). "With whom did He consult? With the souls of the righteous": He "consulted" whether to will creation at all (line 245). That end is "the Holy One desired a dwelling in the lowest", souls in bodies doing Torah and mitzvot (line 245). And there, at the end, the Essence Himself will shine in bounded time: "the limit itself becomes unlimited, which can only be from the Essence" (line 245). Bring it home. You are in the picture the Builder saw before He began. Your day, with its mitzvot in a body in a real place, is the house He wanted to live in. The builder picture breaks at one point: a man builds because he lacks a house. He lacks nothing, and still desired this.

**From inside.** A deep sense of purpose that does not depend on success. The ordinary day becomes the point, not a detour. Some feel wanted in advance.

**At the edge.** Yes. The dwelling below "rose before the beginning of thought", and there "the limit itself becomes unlimited" (line 245). The mind cannot see before the beginning; it can live inside the end.

**Into the day.** Do one mitzvah today in a plain, physical place, and say: "Here is Your house."

## Shaar Kriat Shema §41: two kinds of self-giving; "aveh" and "ahavah" (lines 246-249)

**The movement.** It separates the soul's natural readiness to give itself, which needs no command, from the self-giving of a soul in a body, which is commanded: "you shall love". It then says the second is the one He wanted. The mind learns to prize the harder, embodied giving.

> כי לא נמלך רק בנשמתן של צדיקים שבגופי' ולא בנשמות מצד עצמן
> *For He consulted only with the souls of the righteous in bodies, not with souls as they are in themselves.*

**Contemplating it.** Breadth: "Israel rose in thought" means Israel was there before the beginning of the thought, the one He consulted (lines 246-247). So only Israel can say "Havayah is our God, Havayah is One" and make the unity, from its root in the Essence (line 247). "Make Him king in heaven and earth" is read upward here: letting the directions dissolve into Him (line 248). Step by step: two self-givings. In the first verse, the soul gives itself "into echad" from its root; that is natural and needs no command (line 248).
